\delta_{\xi}S_{\text{ct}}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\sum_{n=0}^{2}\delta_{\xi}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}(K_{(n)}-\lambda_{(n)})\right)=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\sum_{n=0}^{2}\hat{\pi}_{(n)}^{ij}\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\,,

(4.66)

where we have made use of (
4.30
) and followed
127
in putting a hat on the counterterm momenta. This notation indicates they are defined as predetermined local functionals of the off-shell induced fields rather than determined by the asymptotic behaviour of the on-shell fields. We then apply the transformation (
4.54
) and make use of the initial value constraint (
4.20
)
12
12

12

Even though we are working off-shell, we can still apply this constraint to the counterterms as they satisfy this by construction.
to write this term as

\delta_{\xi}S_{\text{ct}}=-2\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\sum_{n=0}^{2}\hat{\pi}_{(n)}^{ij}K_{ij}.

(4.67)

We can now combine equations (
4.64
), (
4.65
) and (
4.67
) and arrive at

\delta_{\xi}S_{\text{ren}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\xi^{\tau}\sqrt{\gamma}\left(R[\gamma]+K_{ij}K^{ij}-K^{2}-6-32\pi G\sum_{n=0}^{2}\hat{\pi}_{(n)}^{ij}K_{ij}\right)\,,

(4.68)

which is our final expression for the variation of the renormalised action. The fact that this is a boundary term reflects the fact that the bulk theory is diffeomorphism invariant, and that we looked for invariance under (a class of) diffeomorphisms. Asymptotic symmetries are the transformations for which (
4.68
) vanishes.

Using (
4.53
), we see that the apparent leading divergence at
\mathcal{O}(e^{3\tau})
in fact vanishes and thus the integrand above has a leading order divergence at
\mathcal{O}(e^{2\tau})
. We thus conclude that in order to preserve the renormalised action
off-shell
we require the falloff on
\xi^{\tau}
to be

\xi^{\tau}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-3\tau})\iff\sigma_{\xi}(x)=0\,,

(4.69)

and thus our ASG is generated by vectors of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi^{\tau}&=\mathcal{O}(e^{-3\tau})\,,\\
\xi^{i}&=\xi^{i}_{(0)}(x)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-5\tau})\,,\end{split}

(4.70)

where
\xi_{(0)}
is an arbitrary vector field. We note that for
on-shell
configurations, the renormalised action is preserved even when
\sigma_{\xi}\neq 0
. This can be seen using the Hamiltonian constraint (
4.19
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{\xi}S_{\text{ren}}&\approx\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\xi^{\tau}\sqrt{\gamma}\left(K_{ij}K^{ij}-K^{2}-16\pi G\sum_{n=0}^{2}\pi_{(n)}^{ij}K_{ij}\right)\\
&=2\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\xi^{\tau}\sqrt{\gamma}\left(\pi^{ij}K_{ij}-\sum_{n=0}^{2}\pi_{(n)}^{ij}K_{ij}\right)\\
&=2\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\xi^{\tau}\sqrt{\gamma}\pi_{(3)}^{ij}K_{ij}=0\,,\end{split}

(4.71)

where in the final equality we used equations (
4.37
),
(
4.38
) and (
4.41
).
This reflects the fact that (
4.52
) are associated with a well-posed variational problem.

We thus found that the (off-shell) action is invariant under the transformations (
4.70
). Then by Noether’s theorem there should exist a corresponding charge that generates these transformations. The transformations (
4.70
) are local transformations and, as such, their associated charges should be zero, and we will confirm this in the next subsection. Non-trivial conserved charges appear when we impose that the
conformal class is fixed
. In deriving the charges by various alternative methods in the forthcoming sections, we will make use of the boundary condition of preservation of the conformal class

\delta\gamma_{ij}\propto\gamma_{ij}\;\;\;\text{on}\;\mathscr{I}^{+}\,,

(4.72)

which forces the following relation on the vector fields
\xi

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi^{\tau}&=\mathcal{O}(e^{-3\tau}),\\
\xi^{i}&=\xi^{i}_{(0)}(x)+\mathcal{O}(e^{-5\tau}),\qquad L_{\xi_{(0)}}\gamma_{ij}=\frac{2}{3}\gamma_{ij}D_{k}\xi^{k}\;\;\;\text{on}\;\mathscr{I}^{+}\,,\end{split}

(4.73)

i.e
the subset of the boundary diffeomorphisms (
4.70
) where
\xi_{(0)}
is a conformal Killing vector (CKV) of
g_{(0)}^{+}
. We note that these conditions are precisely the AldS analogue of the conditions in Appendix B of
127
which define an
asymptotic conformal Killing vector
(ACKV). Not all spacetimes admit such vectors, and some spacetimes may only admit such vectors over a portion of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In such cases (as we will see) one can define gravitational charges that are conserved at these portions and satisfy balance laws outside these portions where the change in the charge is balanced by the net flux of the corresponding current density. These balance laws follow from the invariance of the action under the more general transformations (
4.70
) and the vanishing of the charges that generate them.

4.4
Gravitational charges at
\mathscr{I}^{+}

Having established that the action has an invariance, one may proceed to derive the conserved charges associated with the asymptotic symmetries using Noether’s theorem(s), following analogous steps to the derivation of conserved charges for a QFT on a fixed background. The reason we are able to carry out this computation in complete generality is because we have full control over the asymptotic form of the gravitational field (and this is the case because the cosmological constant is non-zero). We provide two derivations of the charges associated with asymptotic conformal Killing vectors (
4.73
), following closely the analogous derivations in AlAdS presented in
127
, and we will also show that these charges satisfy balance laws when
\xi
is a general vector. We will then show that these charges can also be obtained as Wald Hamiltonians using the covariant phase techniques discussed in Section
3
.

4.4.1
Gravitational charges from Noether’s first theorem

Our starting point is the theory with a conformal class kept fixed, and we
will derive the corresponding gravitational charge formulae using Noether’s first theorem.
To apply Noether’s method, we consider vector fields of the form (
4.73
) and field variations

\delta_{f}\psi=f(\tau,x)\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi\,,

(4.74)

where
f
is an arbitrary function. As usual with Noether’s method, such a variation does not generically preserve the renormalised action (this is the case only when
f
is a constant), although it does preserve the boundary conditions (
4.72
). Following
127
, we will denote with a bar an object that is defined on
\Sigma_{\tau}
, for example
\bar{f}
will denote the pullback of
f
and
\bar{d}
the boundary exterior derivative. As shown in (
4.51
), the general variation of the action takes the form

\delta S_{\text{ren}}=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\delta\psi+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\delta\gamma_{ij}\,,

(4.75)

where we have now included the
\mathbf{E}
term as we work off-shell. Applying the variation (
4.74
) to this we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{f}S_{\text{ren}}&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}f\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}f\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\\
&=-\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}fd(\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{L})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}f\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\mathbf{J}[\xi]-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f(\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{L})+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}f\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\,,\end{split}

(4.76)

where we used (
3.2
) in moving to the second line and integration by parts together with the definition (
3.7
) in the third line. We want to extract the full Noether current by manipulating the above expression in order to show that the integrand is proportional to the derivative of
f
. Clearly the bulk term above is already of the correct form, but the boundary terms require some more work.

In order to correctly manipulate the boundary terms above, we begin by noting from (
4.51
) that the pullback of the symplectic potential takes the form

\left.\bm{\Theta}\right|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}=\delta\mathbf{B}+\sqrt{\gamma}\pi^{ij}_{(3)}\delta\gamma_{ij}\,d\mu+d\mathbf{Y}\,,

(4.77)

where

\mathbf{B}=-\mathbf{L}_{\text{GHY}}-\mathbf{L}_{\text{ct}}\,,

(4.78)

and
\mathbf{Y}
is a
2
-form which is left undetermined in the variational problem as the integral of an exact form over a manifold without boundary (in this case
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
) is zero. Although
\mathbf{Y}
does not affect the variational problem, it will be important in the final expression for the charges. The explicit expression for this “corner term” has appeared in several related works concerning charges in Al(A)dS
4
spacetimes
62
;
4
;
59
;
111
and is often referred to as the
counterterm symplectic potential
, given explicitly via

d\mathbf{Y}=d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)\equiv\delta\mathbf{L}_{\text{ct}}-\delta\gamma_{ij}\frac{\delta\mathbf{L}_{\text{ct}}}{\delta\gamma_{ij}}\,,

(4.79)

and for our counterterm action (
4.33
) we have

\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)=-\bm{\Theta}^{(3)}_{\text{EH}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}\varepsilon_{ijk}\left(D_{l}\delta\gamma^{li}-D^{i}(\gamma_{lm}\delta\gamma^{lm})\right)dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}\,,

(4.80)

a result which we note is special to
d=4
bulk dimensions.
13
13

13

In higher dimensions the counterterm action would be supplemented by higher curvature terms
69
and it would be interesting to examine the analogous modifications to the symplectic potential.

In what follows, we will find it more convenient to work with

\left.\bm{\Theta}\right|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)=\delta\mathbf{B}+\sqrt{\gamma}\pi^{ij}_{(3)}\delta\gamma_{ij}\,d\mu\,,

(4.81)

which corresponds to utilising the inherent ambiguity present in
\bm{\Theta}
in always being able to add an exact form
104
. Replacing the generic variation
\delta
with
\delta_{\xi}
where
\xi
is a vector in our asymptotic symmetry group (
4.73
) gives

\left.\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)=\delta_{\xi}\mathbf{B}+\sqrt{\gamma}\pi^{ij}_{(3)}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\,d\mu\,.

(4.82)

With this established, we can now perform the following manipulations of the variation of the action (
4.76
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{f}S_{\text{ren}}&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\mathbf{J}[\xi]-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f[\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{L}]+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}f\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\mathbf{J}[\xi]-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}f\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\mathbf{J}[\xi]-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f[\delta_{\xi}\mathbf{B}+d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)]\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\left\{\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right\}-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f\delta_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\,,\end{split}

(4.83)

where we made use of the fact that
\xi
as defined in (
4.73
) is tangential to
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
in going from the first to second line, equation (
4.82
) in the second to third line and Stokes’ theorem in the final line. We note that

\mathbf{B}=-\mathbf{L}_{\text{GHY}}-\mathbf{L}_{\text{ct}}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\cdot\frac{1}{3!}\left[K-\sum_{n=0}^{2}(K_{(n)}-\lambda_{(n)})\right]\epsilon_{ijk}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}\,,

(4.84)

which is covariant with respect to boundary diffeomorphisms (of which
\xi
is) and thus

\delta_{\xi}\mathbf{B}=L_{\xi}\mathbf{B}=\bar{d}i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\,,

(4.85)

where we used
L_{\xi}=i_{\xi}\bar{d}+\bar{d}i_{\xi}
and the fact that
\mathbf{B}
is a top-form of the boundary. Using this, we can further manipulate (
4.83
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{f}S_{\text{ren}}&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\left\{\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right\}-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}f\bar{d}i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\left\{\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right\}+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\bar{d}f\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\left\{\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right\}+\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge df\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\\
&=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}df\wedge\left\{\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\right\}\,,\end{split}

(4.86)

where we introduced the
Poincaré dual
of
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
in
M_{\tau_{0}}
,
\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
, a 1-form with
\delta
-function support on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
. We have thus established that the variation of the off-shell renormalised action is proportional to the derivative of
f
.

In order to construct the current, we now follow the usual argument of Noether’s theorem in noticing that the on-shell variation of the renormalised action is

\delta_{f}S_{\text{ren}}\approx 0\,,

(4.87)

which can be seen from the first line of (
4.76
) using
\mathbf{E}=0
together with the fact that
\xi
is an ACKV (
4.73
) and
\pi_{(3)}^{ij}
is traceless (
4.38
). This allows us to identify the Noether current as

\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]\equiv\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\approx d(\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi))-\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\,,

(4.88)

where we used (
3.9
) in the on-shell evaluation.

In order to construct the Noether charge, we will need to integrate the current (
4.88
) over a suitable hypersurface
C
which intersects a cross-section of the conformal boundary. The natural choice of
C
made in both asymptotically flat
165
;
104
;
105
;
164
and AlAdS
127
;
59
is a spacelike hypersurface which represents an “instant of time” and
\partial C
is the intersection of
C
with the conformal boundary. In the case of AldS spacetimes, “instants of time” are now compact manifolds and thus do not serve as suitable hypersurfaces due to their lack of boundary. Due to this, in AldS spacetimes we will take
C
to be a
timelike
slice in spacetime, which intersects the conformal boundary as shown in Figure
3
.

We note that different choices of hypersurfaces
C
have appeared in the literature: our choice of timelike
C
is in keeping with the work of
59
;
114
, whereas
14
;
15
;
16
;
4
;
138
utilise
spacelike
hypersurfaces in various contexts. We note that everywhere spacelike hypersurfaces are not possible
for charges defined at cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as such a hypersurface must necessarily be compact and thus cannot intersect the conformal boundary in a non-trivial cross-section. This tells us that at least somewhere on the hypersurface, the causal character must change from spacelike. Another possible choice used in
47
;
46
;
49
is to use
null cones
which intersect
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We shall see that for one-ended hypersurfaces
C
the charges will only depend on
\partial C
and thus the bulk causal nature of
C
will be irrelevant. We will show in Section
4.5
that our choice of timelike
C
naturally allows one to include the contribution from
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. For now, we restrict consideration to the one-ended case and define the Noether charge as

Q_{\xi}=\int_{C}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]\approx\int_{\partial C}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\,.

(4.89)

Figure 3:
Penrose diagram showing the setup which we use to construct the charges for AldS spacetimes. The timelike hypersurface
C
shown in red is the hypersurface which we integrate the Noether current
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
over in equation (
4.89
). The charge will be defined as an integral over the surface
\partial C^{+}=C\cap\mathscr{I}^{+}
. For comparison, we also include a spacelike “instant of time” hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau}
, which is compact and does not intersect
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

It remains to show that the charge is invariant along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, a notion we will refer to as
radial invariance
. To show this, we first use Stokes’ theorem to re-write the difference of charges as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}}-\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}}=\int_{C_{2}}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]-\int_{C_{1}}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]=\int_{R}d\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]-\int_{B_{12}\subset\mathscr{I}^{+}}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}[\xi]\,,\end{split}

(4.90)

where the setup is depicted in Figure
4
. Taking this expression on-shell we find

Figure 4:
Penrose diagram displaying an AldS
4
spacetime with radially invariant charges. The charges associated with the timelike slices
C_{1,2}
are equivalent.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}}-\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}}&\approx-\int_{\partial R}\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\\
&=\int_{C_{1}}\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}-\int_{C_{2}}\rho(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\wedge i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\\
&=\int_{\partial C_{1}}i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}-\int_{\partial C_{2}}i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\\
&=-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{J}[\xi]-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\bar{d}i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\\
&=-\frac{3}{32\pi G}\int_{B_{12}}d^{3}x\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}g_{(3)}^{+\;ij}\delta_{\xi}g^{+}_{(0)ij}=0\,,\end{split}

(4.91)

where in the last equality we used the fact that
\xi
is ACKV, so
\delta_{\xi}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\propto g^{+}_{(0)ij}
and
the final expression vanishes since
g_{(3)}^{+\;ij}
is traceless on-shell. This demonstrates that the Noether charges are radially invariant.

We now relax the condition that
\xi
is an ACKV and only assume
(
4.70
). One may still define the charges using (
4.89
) but this charge is no longer conserved - it rather satisfies a flux-balance law,

\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}}-\left.Q_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}}=-\int_{B^{+}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}}\,,

(4.92)

where

\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}}=-\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\nabla^{+}_{i}\xi^{(0)}_{j}\,d\mu\,,

(4.93)

is the flux of the current density associated with
Q_{\xi}
.
This balance equation follows from the same steps given in (
4.91
), except that now the expression in the last step does not vanish but rather defines the flux.
In writing the last term in (
4.91
) as in (
4.93
) we used (
4.41
).
In the next subsection we will give another perspective around this flux-balance law, and we also present another derivation later using covariant phase space methods.

Note that (
4.92
) implies that the gravitational charge is conserved if and only if the flux vanishes. If
\xi
is an ACKV,
\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}}=0
point-wise and we recover the conservation law we derived earlier. However, as the r.h.s. of (
4.92
) involves the integral of
\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}}
, it would suffice for
\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}}
to be exact over the compact directions of
B_{12}
in order for
Q_{\xi}
to be conserved. We will discuss examples of such more general conservation laws in Section
6
.

4.4.2
Gravitational charges from Noether’s second theorem

Now we will consider a second derivation of the gravitational charges using Noether’s second theorem. As in the previous subsection, we start by considering the case of keeping the conformal class fixed. In order to derive the charges via this approach we consider vector fields of the form (
4.73
) and variations

\delta_{h}\psi=\mathcal{L}_{h\xi}\psi\,,

(4.94)

where
h=h(x)
is an arbitrary function on the boundary. We note that (
4.94
) will preserve the renormalised action off-shell

0=\delta_{h}S_{\text{ren}}=\int_{M_{\tau_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\delta_{h}\psi+\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\delta_{h}\gamma_{ij}\,,

(4.95)

since
h\xi
is of the form (
4.70
), but will not preserve the boundary conditions (
4.72
). In order to derive the charges, we note that on-shell we have

0=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\delta_{h}\gamma_{ij}=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\,\sqrt{\gamma}\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\left(hL_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}+2\xi_{i}D_{j}h\right)\,,

(4.96)

and now performing integration by parts on the second term and using the fact that
h
is an arbitrary function we have

\displaystyle-2D_{i}\left(\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\xi_{j}\right)=-\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}=0\,,

(4.97)

where in the second equality we used the condition in (
4.73
) that
\xi_{(0)}
is a CKV and
\pi_{(3)}^{ij}
is traceless. We thus reach the conclusion that

\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}=-\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}\cap C}\pi_{(3)j}^{\phantom{(3)}i}\xi^{j}_{(0)}\epsilon_{ikl}\,dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\,,

(4.98)

is a radially invariant charge associated with a boundary conformal Killing vector
\xi_{(0)}
.

The expression given above seems at first to be different to the charge expression (
4.89
) derived using Noether’s first theorem. Such a discrepancy parallels the AlAdS case studied in
127
, where the charges derived using each method were shown to be equivalent assuming some mild symmetry for the spacetimes, most importantly the stationarity of the AlAdS conformal boundary. As our analysis in the AldS case has followed that work closely, one might now expect a similar result to hold. As it turns out, the “corner improvement” we introduced using the counterterm symplectic potential
\mathbf{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}
is sufficient to remove the requirement to impose
any
symmetry (beyond the existence of ACKVs) on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in order to have equivalence of (
4.89
) and (
4.98
). This will become apparent after we present a third derivation in the next subsection using covariant phase space methods, but first we will discuss what happens if we relax the condition that
\xi
is a ACKV.

We now consider the case
\xi
satisfies the more general fall-off condition (
4.70
). Equation (
4.96
) still holds and we conclude,

-2D_{i}\left(\pi_{(3)}^{ij}\xi_{j}\right)=-\pi_{(3)}^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\,,

(4.99)

but the r.h.s. is no longer zero. Integrating over
B_{12}
in the boundary (see Figure
4
) and using the fact that the l.h.s. is a total derivative we obtain the flux-balance law in (
4.92
).

4.4.3
Wald Hamiltonians at
\mathscr{I}^{+}

In this section we derive the expressions for the Wald Hamiltonians (
3.12
) on AldS
4
spacetimes associated to
one-ended
hypersurfaces whose end is a cross-section of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. To do this, we will first determine the subset of the well-posed boundary conditions for which the integrability condition (
3.17
) is satisfied. We will compute the spacetime vector fields which preserve such boundary conditions and use these to provide an alternative definition of the ASG at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
(
4.73
). We will derive the explicit expression for the Hamiltonians and show that these are radially invariant quantities along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In this process we will demonstrate that they are equivalent to both (
4.89
) and (
4.98
).

We will then consider the case of non-existence of the Hamiltonians
i.e.
when (
3.17
) is not satisfied. This will be the relevant case in order to describe AldS
4
spacetimes with non-trivial gravitational radiation. In order to construct the charges in such a setting, we will construct the modified Hamiltonians (
3.19
) and give a formula for the radial flux between neighbouring charges along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The results of this section reproduce several results of the prior literature
4
;
59
;
114
although using the Hamiltonian approach to the holographic renormalisation provides a fresh perspective on several aspects of the derivation.

Existence and asymptotic symmetries

It was shown in
105
that the existence of a well-posed variational problem is intimately related to the integrability of Hamilton’s equations on phase space (
3.12
). In this section we will determine the subset of the boundary conditions (
4.52
) which satisfy the integrability condition (
3.17
) and thus give rise to a Wald Hamiltonian of the form (
3.15
). We will then compute the generic expression for these Hamiltonians at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

Applying the well-posed boundary conditions (
4.52
) to (
4.81
) we find

\left.\bm{\Theta}\right|_{\mathscr{I^{+}}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}=\delta\mathbf{B}+d(*\bm{X})\,,

(4.100)

where

X_{i}=-\langle T_{ij}\rangle^{\mathscr{I^{+}}}\zeta^{j}\,.

(4.101)

This structure is the same as that found in equation (11) of
105
, which goes on to prove that the integrability condition (
3.15
) is satisfied when
*\bm{X}=0
,
although this is not the most generic choice which allows for existence of a Hamiltonian. In order to determine the latter, we begin by evaluating the symplectic current (
3.4
) pulled back to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
using (
4.81
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\bm{\omega}\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}&=\left(-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}\left(\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\right)\delta_{2}(g^{+}_{(0)ij})-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\right)d\mu\\
&=\left(-\delta_{1}\left(\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)i}\zeta_{2j}\right)-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\right)d\mu\,,\end{split}

(4.102)

which we see is generically non-zero for boundary diffeomorphisms generated by
\zeta_{1,2}
. In order to satisfy the existence criterion (
3.17
) at the level of the integrand vanishing (when
\xi
is not tangential to
C\cap\mathscr{I}^{+}
) we must have

\nabla^{+}_{(0)(i}\zeta_{j)}=\frac{1}{3}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)k}\zeta^{k}\,,

(4.103)

i.e.

\zeta
must be a conformal Killing vector of
g^{+}_{(0)}
. Such a requirement means that (
4.52
) becomes

\delta g^{+}_{(0)ij}=2\left(\sigma+\frac{1}{3}\nabla^{+}_{(0)k}\zeta^{k}\right)g^{+}_{(0)ij}=2\tilde{\sigma}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\,,

(4.104)

hence the boundary conditions which lead to both a well-posed variational problem and existence of a Hamiltonian are the preservation of the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We also note that due to (
4.103
), the pullback of the symplectic potential (
4.77
) becomes

\left.\bm{\Theta}\right|_{\mathscr{I^{+}}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)=\delta\mathbf{B}\,.

(4.105)

Finally, we want to determine which vectors in (
4.56
) respect this existence criterion. The action of these vector fields on the representative of the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
can be determined by extracting the
\mathcal{O}(e^{2\tau})=\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{-2})
term of the transformation of the full metric tensor
\delta_{\xi}g_{ij}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{ij}
and reads

\delta_{\xi}g^{+}_{(0)ij}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}^{k}}g^{+}_{(0)ij}+2\sigma_{\xi}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\,,

(4.106)

which we notice precisely matches the general transformation rule which ensures a well-posed variational problem (
4.52
). In order to define a Hamiltonian, the argument given in equation (
4.103
) ensures that
\xi_{(0)}^{i}
must be a conformal Killing vector of
g^{+}_{(0)ij}

\nabla^{+}_{(0)(i}\xi^{(0)}_{j)}=\frac{1}{3}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)k}\xi_{(0)}^{k}\,,

(4.107)

and we also note from (
4.56
) that

\sigma_{\xi}=0\,,

(4.108)

ensures that we use field independent vector fields
\delta\xi=0
as considered in
165
;
104
;
105
;
164
. We have thus arrived at the ASG of ACKVs as defined in (
4.73
), although we note that this time we derived the group by demanding existence of a Hamiltonian associated to field independent vectors
\xi
, rather than by invariance of the renormalised off-shell action (
4.34
) and the choice of boundary conditions (
4.72
).

If we instead attempted to use vectors with
\sigma_{\xi}\neq 0
as our asymptotic symmetries, then the appearance of the inverse metric in the
\xi^{i}
component of (
4.56
) would force one to consider “field dependent” symmetries where
\delta\xi\neq 0
and the charge algebra becomes soft. Such a formalism is a topic of some study, (see
e.g.
related formulae in
63
;
54
) but we do not consider such cases here.

Radially invariant Hamiltonians

With the asymptotic symmetries established, we are now ready to give an explicit expression for the Hamiltonians (
3.12
) of AldS
spacetimes associated with the vector fields
\xi
in (
4.56
) satisfying properties (
4.107
) and (
4.108
). For convenience, we recall Hamilton’s equations of motion on phase space

\delta H_{\xi}=\Omega_{C}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)=\int_{C}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)=\int_{\partial C}\delta\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)\,,

(4.109)

where
C
is again a timelike slice as displayed in Figure
3
. We recall that the equation above has been written without any consideration of the ambiguities present in the definition of
\bm{\Theta}
. Since we employ the shift (
4.81
), the variation of our Hamiltonian is given by
104

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta H_{\xi}&=\int_{C}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\\
&=\int_{\partial C}\delta\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\\
&=\int_{\partial C}\delta\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-\delta\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi,\psi)+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\delta\psi,\psi)\\
&=\int_{\partial C}\delta\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-\delta\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi,\psi)+i_{\xi}d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\delta\psi,\psi)+di_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\delta\psi,\psi)\\
&=\int_{\partial C}\delta\left[\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi,\psi)\right]-i_{\xi}\left[\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\delta\psi,\psi)\right]\,,\end{split}

(4.110)

where in the first line we used Stokes’ theorem, in the second the definition of the symplectic current, (
3.4
), in the third,
{\cal L}_{\xi}=i_{\xi}d+di_{\xi}
, and in final line we packaged the terms together in order to demonstrate how the Noether charge and the symplectic potential transform respectively. Notice that we have ignored the contribution of the
d
-exact term as we assume that
\partial(\partial C)=\emptyset
. This term would have to be included if one considers a spacetime whose cross-sections contain non-trivial boundary, for example the C-metric solutions
112
where the contribution of such terms was considered in the AlAdS case
111
.

With the hypersurface
C
and the vector
\xi
established, it remains to compute the explicit formulae for the charges (
4.110
). With the explicit form of Hamilton’s equations on phase space now derived, we extract the Hamiltonian directly from integrating the right hand side of (
4.110
). To do this, we note the well-known result for the Noether charge of general relativity

\mathbf{Q}[\xi]=-*\mathbf{\Xi}[\xi]\,,

(4.111)

where

\Xi_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\nabla_{[\mu}\xi_{\nu]}\,.

(4.112)

Now using the gauge (
4.13
), the Christoffel symbols (
4.22
) and the asymptotic symmetry conditions (
4.73
) for
\xi
we find the relevant components are

\nabla^{[i}\xi^{\tau]}=K^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{5})\,,

(4.113)

and so

\mathbf{Q}[\xi]=-\frac{1}{8\pi G}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}\epsilon_{ijk\tau}\nabla^{[k}\xi^{\tau]}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}K^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+})\,,

(4.114)

which gives us the first term in the integral (
4.110
).

In order to derive the second term we note

-\left(*_{3}\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}\right)^{i}=\left(*_{3}\bm{\Theta}^{(3)}_{\text{EH}}\right)^{i}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\left(D^{i}(\gamma_{jk}\delta\gamma^{jk})-D_{j}\delta\gamma^{ij}\right)=v^{i}_{(2)}=v^{i}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{5})\,,

(4.115)

where
v^{i}
is defined in (
4.6
) and the subscript indicates dilatation weight (see Section
4.1
) and hence

\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta_{\xi}\gamma)=-*_{3}*_{3}\bm{\Theta}^{(3)}_{\text{EH}}(\gamma,\delta_{\xi}\gamma)=-\frac{1}{2!}v_{(2)}^{k}\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,.

(4.116)

For the third term we use (
4.105
) to write

i_{\xi}\left[\bm{\Theta}-d\bm{\Theta_{\text{ct}}}\right]=\delta i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\delta\left\{\left[K-\sum_{n=0}^{2}(K_{(n)}-\lambda_{(n)})\right]\epsilon_{kij}\right\}\xi^{k}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+})\,,

(4.117)

and thus the Hamiltonian (
4.110
) takes the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{\xi}&=\int_{\partial C}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi,\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}(\psi)\\
&=-\frac{1}{8\pi G}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}\int_{\partial C}\left(K^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}+v_{(2)}^{k}-\left\{K-\sum_{n=0}^{2}(K_{(n)}-\lambda_{(n)})\right\}\xi^{k}\right)\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\\
&=-\int_{\partial C}\left(\pi^{\phantom{(3)}k}_{(3)l}\xi^{l}+\frac{1}{16\pi G}\left(v_{(2)}^{k}+\sum_{n=0}^{2}(K^{\phantom{(n)}k}_{(n)l}-\lambda_{(n)}\delta^{k}_{l})\xi^{l}\right)\right)\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,,\end{split}

(4.118)

where the extra minus sign which appears in front of the charge is due to the fact that the orientation of
\partial C
when embedded in
C
is opposite that of the “natural” orientation on surfaces in AldS
4
spacetimes and we employed the definition of the conjugate momenta (
4.18
) in going to the final line. The naïve conclusion from counting dilatation weights of these terms is that the first term is finite and the second is divergent at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In fact, the second term vanishes, a proof of which is given in Appendix
C
. The inclusion of the counterterm symplectic potential is important in the vanishing of the second term and allows for a relaxation of assumption
ii)
of Lemma 4.1 in
127
, where the term was not included and the additional assumption of the metric
\gamma_{ij}
having an isometry direction orthogonal to the hypersurface
\partial C
was required. Such a relaxation is only complete for
d=4
, and would be interesting to pursue in higher spacetime dimensions. Looking ahead to the next subsection, we also note that the main use of relaxaing this assumption is in being able to extend the notion of a phase space Hamiltonian to the cases where
\not{\exists}
ACKVs, as many of the known solutions which possess ACKVs (
e.g.
Kerr-Newman-dS
4
) already satisfy the assumptions of
127
.

We summarise the main result as follows: for an AldS
4
spacetime, the Wald Hamiltonians corresponding to the ACKVs
\xi
are given by

H_{\xi}=-\int_{\partial C}\pi^{\phantom{(3)}k}_{(3)l}\xi^{l}\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C}T^{k}_{l}\xi_{(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,.

(4.119)

where we used (
4.41
) and the fact that the only terms which contribute are those at leading order. We also note that by equations (
4.118
) and (
4.119
) the Wald Hamiltonians are equivalent to
both
Noether charges
Q_{\xi}
as defined in (
4.89
) and
\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}
defined in (
4.98
). This means that all three derivations of the charges ar
\mathscr{I}^{+}
give the same value and are equivalent.

The fact that the Hamiltonians are the same as the Noether charge also means they are radially invariant by equation (
4.91
). We can equivalently show this using (
3.18
), where the difference between two charges defined at cross-sections
\partial C_{1},\partial C_{2}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\delta H_{\xi}\right|_{\partial C_{2}}-\left.\delta H_{\xi}\right|_{\partial C_{1}}&=-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\\
&=\delta\int_{B_{12}}\left(\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)(j}\xi^{(0)}_{i)}\right)d\mu\\
&=0\,,\end{split}

(4.120)

where we used the conformal Killing equation (
4.107
) in the final step.

A final important note concerning the charges derived using this technique is that they appear to suffer from ambiguities arising from the integration of Hamilton’s equations on the phase space. If
H_{\xi}
satisfies (
4.110
), then so does

H^{\prime}_{\xi}=H_{\xi}+\int_{\partial C}H^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}\epsilon_{ikl}dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\,,\qquad\delta\left(\int_{\partial C}H^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}\epsilon_{ikl}dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\right)=0\,,

(4.121)

where the components of
H^{i}_{j}
are analytic functionals of the intrinsic quantities defined on the boundary. The requirement for the second term to be in the Kernel of
\delta
enforces constraints on
H^{i}_{j}
which depend on the boundary conditions. For the boundary conditions (
4.72
) (equivalent to (
4.104
) which give existence of a Hamiltonian), we have up to total derivatives

\delta\left(H^{i}_{j}\sqrt{\gamma}\right)=\sqrt{\gamma}\delta H^{i}_{j}+3\tilde{\sigma}H^{i}_{j}\sqrt{\gamma}=0\iff\delta H^{i}_{j}=-3\tilde{\sigma}H^{i}_{j}\,,

(4.122)

i.e.

H^{i}_{j}
has dilatation weight
3
. By the requirement of radial invariance of the charge
H_{\xi}^{\prime}
along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
we also have

D_{i}\left(H^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}\right)=\xi^{j}D_{i}H^{i}_{j}+H^{i}_{j}D_{i}\xi^{j}=\xi^{j}D_{i}H^{i}_{j}+\frac{1}{3}H^{i}_{i}D_{k}\xi^{k}=0\,,

(4.123)

where we used the fact that
\xi
is an ACKV of the boundary (
4.73
). The solution to the above equation is

D_{i}H^{i}_{j}=H^{i}_{i}=0\,,

(4.124)

i.e.

H^{i}_{j}
is traceless and conserved. In
d=4
there is no quantity which satisfies this property other than
\pi_{(3)j}^{\phantom{(3)}i}
.
14
14

14

This is no longer be the case if we allow for parity odd conserved tensor: the Cotton-York tensor,
C_{ij}=\epsilon_{i}{}^{kl}D_{k}(R_{lj}-\frac{1}{4}Rg_{lj})
satisfies the conditions (
4.124
). Such term would be produced if we include the Pontryagin term
in the bulk action.
. In fact there is no local parity-even diffeomorphism covariant quantity constructed from a metric that satisfies (
4.122
) even before we impose any additional conditions (such as (
4.124
)). Note that
\pi_{(3)j}^{\phantom{(3)}i}
is not a local function of
g_{(0)}
, and it already appears in the expression (
4.119
) for the radially invariant Hamiltonian and thus the only ambiguity present is shifting the overall numerical factor multiplying the charge. The radially invariant Hamiltonian is thus defined unambiguously once one chooses a suitable normalisation of the ACKV
\xi
.

Fluxes - Modified Hamiltonians

We have constructed in equation (
4.119
) the generic expression for the Wald Hamiltonian at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
of an AldS
4
spacetime associated with an ACKV
\xi
. A shortcoming of this formula is that it does not apply to spacetimes which fail to admit the required asymptotic isometries at (at least some portion of)
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The natural conclusion is that such spacetimes do not possess Wald Hamiltonians which are finite and radially invariant at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and must instead exhibit non-trivial gravitational radiation reaching
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In order to describe the presence of this radiation, we will now construct the modified Hamiltonians following the procedure described in Section
3.2
. The formulae we derive will be applicable to a portion of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
where
\nexists
an ACKV, although we will be interested in cases where the spacetime does admit an ACKV in a particular portion or limit of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, an explicit example of which we will discuss in Section
6
.

We begin by noting that for a spacetime which does not possess an ACKV, one generically has

g_{(3)}^{+\;ij}\delta_{\xi}g^{+}_{(0)ij}=g_{(3)}^{+\;ij}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}^{k}}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\neq 0\,,

(4.125)

as there is no conformal Killing vector
\xi_{(0)}
. Recalling equations (
4.77
) and (
4.103
), we note that a sufficient condition for existence of a Hamiltonian was that the boundary vector field
\zeta
in the well-posed boundary conditions (
4.52
) was a CKV although now such a vector field does not exist!

We revisit the pullback of the symplectic current to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
(
4.102
)

\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)=\left(-\delta_{1}\left(\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)i}\zeta_{2j}\right)-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\right)d\mu\,,

(4.126)

and we note that this is now generically non-zero and the existence criterion (
3.17
) fails. Clearly we need to apply the modification procedure in order to define a Wald Hamiltonian. In order to identify a suitable candidate for the modification term in (
3.19
), we note that the pullback of the symplectic potential is now given by

\left.\bm{\Theta}\right|_{\mathscr{I^{+}}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\gamma,\delta\gamma)=\delta\mathbf{B}-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\delta g^{+}_{(0)ij}d\mu\,,

(4.127)

and thus the pullback of the symplectic current is

\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}\left(\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\delta_{2}g^{+}_{(0)ij}\right)-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(4.128)

allowing us to identify a candidate for the modification term as

\bm{\theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\delta g^{+}_{(0)ij}d\mu+\delta\mathbf{W}(\psi)\,,

(4.129)

where we have included the ambiguity term
\mathbf{W}
which can be fixed with some physical criteria. The criteria discussed in
114
of locality, conformal invariance and analyticity uniquely fix

\mathbf{W}=0\,,

(4.130)

and thus with this choice one finds the modified Hamiltonian as defined in (
3.19
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta\mathcal{H}_{\xi}&=\int_{\partial C}\delta\left[\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right]-i_{\xi}\left[\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta\psi)-\bm{\theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)\right]\\
&=\delta\int_{\partial C}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{B}\,,\end{split}

(4.131)

which is in agreement with (
4.118
). We note that the flux between neighbouring hypersurfaces (
3.21
) is given by

\left.\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\right|_{\partial C_{2}}-\left.\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\right|_{\partial C_{1}}=-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{\theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)=\int_{B_{12}}\sqrt{g^{+}_{(0)}}T^{ij}\nabla^{+}_{(0)(i}\xi^{(0)}_{j)}\,d\mu\,,

(4.132)

which is again clearly non-zero due to the absence of CKVs. A Penrose diagram depicting this flux is shown in Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
Penrose diagram displaying an AldS
4
spacetime with gravitational radiation reaching
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Due to the radiation, the Hamiltonians associated with the timelike slices
C_{1,2}
are not equivalent, with their difference given by the flux formula (
4.132
).

For the modified Hamiltonian (
4.131
) there is again a possibility of an ambiguous term arising in the kernel of
\delta
. This equation has to be examined separately to the radially invariant case as the boundary conditions are different. The possible ambiguity is of the form

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\xi}=\mathcal{H}_{\xi}+\int_{\partial C}\mathcal{H}^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}\epsilon_{ikl}dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\,,\qquad\delta\left(\int_{\partial C}\mathcal{H}^{i}_{j}\xi^{j}\epsilon_{ikl}dx^{k}\wedge dx^{l}\right)=0\,,

(4.133)

where now we do not fix the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}
and
\xi
is no longer an ACKV (as the boundary metric does not admit such isometries). We only require that
\xi
satisfies the fall-off conditions (
4.70
), and thus they act on the boundary as local diffeomorphisms. This means that any local diffeomorphism covariant tensor
\mathcal{H}^{i}_{j}
provides such ambiguity. Imposing finiteness, the tensor should have
dilatation weight
3
(dilatation weight
(d-1)
in general dimension), and as we already remarked earlier (below (
4.124
)) there is no such (parity even) term in pure gravity when
d=4
. We also refer to
45
;
114
for complementary discussion about this point.

Comment on different approaches to defining charges

In the previous sections we have utilised three different approaches to derive the charges defined at cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in an AldS
4
spacetime. We now summarise the main points and advantages/drawbacks of each method:

Noether’s first theorem

This method is the most direct analogue of derivation of conserved charges in field theory.
The method relies on considering field variations which do not preserve the off-shell action but do preserve the boundary conditions (
4.73
). This approach allows for a derivation of radially invariant charges along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Relaxing the boundary conditions to (
4.70
) yields a flux-balance equations and a flux formula.

Noether’s second theorem

This method relies on considering field variations which do preserve the action but do not preserve the boundary conditions (
4.73
). The charges derived in this approach (
4.98
) are equivalent to those of the first method (
4.89
) and are thus also radially invariant along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. If we relax the boundary conditions to (
4.70
) then the transforms are represent the local invariance of the off-shell action. This is the formulation that provides the fastest route to the final formulae, including the flux-balance law.

Wald Hamiltonians

In order to satisfy existence of the Hamiltonians as introduced in
165
;
104
;
105
we showed in equation (
4.107
) that the spacetime must admit ACKVs at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The expression for the charges associated with the ACKVs are given in (
4.119
) and they are invariant along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

In order to generalise the construction of Wald Hamiltonians to spacetimes without ACKVs at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, we used the modification procedure first outlined in
164
. This procedure results in a charge expression (
4.131
) which is the same as (
4.119
), although now there is a non-zero flux between neighbouring charges, with the outgoing flux given in equation (
4.132
). We note that in order to apply this procedure it was crucial to include the contributions arising from the “corner term”
\mathbf{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}
introduced by variation of the counterterm action. Had we ignored this term as in the AlAdS case studied in
127
then our expression for the radially invariant Hamiltonian (
4.119
) would still have been valid, although when applied to spacetimes without ACKVs one would find a divergent Hamiltonian as the proof of finiteness given in Appendix
C
would no longer apply. This divergence is precisely cured by the inclusion of the corner terms.

In this article we are primarily interested in the case of pure AldS
4
gravity,
i.e.
with no matter present, where we have just shown that nothing non-trivial appears in the Kernel of
\delta
for both the radially invariant and modified Hamiltonians. However, such ambiguities are present in other situations (
e.g.

d>4
,
d=4
with matter), see the discussion in
127
. It would interesting to understand how to fix this ambiguity in general, see
45
for work in this direction.

In conclusion, all three approaches are equivalent, both when describing radially invariant quantities at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as well as producing a flux formula which describes the passage of gravitational waves in AldS
4
spacetimes. The flux-balance law comes from relaxing the boundary conditions from (
4.73
) to (
4.70
) for the Noether methods and gives the same result as the Wald-Zoupas modification procedure
164
for the covariant phase space Hamiltonians. For clarity, we will work in the language of the covariant phase space for the remainder of this article.

4.5
Past contribution to the charges

In the previous sections we considered the Wald Hamiltonians associated to one-ended timelike hypersurfaces
C
which end at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Despite the global nature being different between AldS and AlAdS, the local structural form of the AldS
4
Hamiltonians inherits much from the analogous results in AlAdS
4
spacetimes
127
. In this section we will now discuss one of the important differences present in AldS vs AlAdS spacetimes: the causal structure of AldS
4
spacetimes indicates that one-ended hypersurfaces are not sufficient to fully describe the effects of gravitational radiation and one should instead consider
two-ended

C
upon which to define the Hamiltonians. This will lead one to consider an additional contribution to the charge arising from the
past end
of the timelike hypersurface
C
.

In order to motivate this additional consideration, we consider the simple examples of pure (A)dS
4
. In AdS
4
, a partial Cauchy spacelike hypersurface
C
has one boundary component, namely
\partial C=C\cap\mathscr{I}
, as the conformal boundary is connected. On the other hand, in dS
4
, a timelike hypersurface
C
necessarily has two boundary components
\partial C=(C\cap\mathscr{I}^{+})\sqcup(C\cap\mathscr{I}^{-})
, as the conformal boundary consists of two disconnected components. As we thus need to consider two-ended
C
even in pure dS
4
, a consideration of the past ends of
C
for all AldS
4
spacetimes is a necessary ingredient to fully understand their charges.

In this section we will develop the technology for the past contributions to the charges, showing how such considerations are essential in determining
conserved quantities
in AldS spacetimes, where
conserved
now means under
time evolution
. We will pay attention to the cases of the past end being both a cut of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
as well as a generic interior spacetime hypersurface. The second case is important in understanding both quasi-local charges as well as the notion of a two ended
C
when
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is not present, for example in the Robinson-Trautman de Sitter spacetime
141
;
154
;
29
which we will discuss in more detail in Section
6
.

4.5.1
Contribution at
\mathscr{I}^{-}

We begin with the case of the past end of
C
being a cut of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. As before, we want to investigate the differences between Hamiltonians associated with these timelike hypersurfaces, leading us to consider the scenario depicted in Figure
6
.

Figure 6:
Penrose diagram of an AldS spacetime where the timelike hypersurfaces
C_{1,2}
stretch between
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}

We recall that in neighbourhoods of both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
one can use separate Starobinsky expansions
153
to write the spacetime metric,
i.e.
near
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
one has

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{\pm}^{2}}\left(-d\rho_{\pm}^{2}+(g^{\pm}_{(0)ij}+\rho_{\pm}^{2}g^{\pm}_{(2)ij}+\rho_{\pm}^{3}g^{\pm}_{(3)ij}+\ldots)dx_{\pm}^{i}dx_{\pm}^{j}\right)\,,

(4.134)

where the coordinates are not required to overlap at all in their respective regions of validity. The power of this result is that one can apply many of the results that we have already proven at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
directly to
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, namely that applying the procedure of holographic renormalisation at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
and demanding asymptotic symmetries consisting of ACKVs at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. At each boundary of the spacetime, we define the energy-momentum tensors
T_{ij}^{(\pm)}
as

T_{ij}^{\pm}=-\frac{3\ell}{16\pi G}g^{(3)\pm}_{ij}\,.

(4.135)

In order to consider the charges depicted in Figure
6
, we require existence of a
double ACKV
: a bulk vector field
\xi
which satisfies
\xi|_{\mathscr{I}^{\pm}}=\xi^{\pm}_{(0)}
where
\xi^{\pm}_{(0)}
are conformal Killing vectors of
g^{\pm}_{(0)}
respectively. In order to examine the past contribution to the charges, we consider the closed form

\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)=\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-d\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\,,

(4.136)

where we note that
\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}
recieves contributions from the counterterm actions that one adds on both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. The precise form of the
\mathscr{I}^{-}
term can be derived using the mappings in (
E.7
) but will not be explicitly needed in our analysis. Employing Stokes’ theorem

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{R}d\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=\int_{\partial R}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)\,,

(4.137)

and taking
R
to be as depicted in Figure
6
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{C_{2}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)+\int_{B_{12}^{+}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=\int_{C_{1}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)+\int_{B_{12}^{-}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)\,,

(4.138)

and now we note that
\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)|_{B_{12}^{\pm}}=0
by virtue of
\xi
being an ACKV of both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. This simplifies the above result to

\int_{C_{1}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)-\int_{C_{2}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=0\,,

(4.139)

which can be recast in terms of the Hamiltonians using (
4.110
) and an integration over the phase space

H_{\xi}|_{C_{1}}-H_{\xi}|_{C_{2}}=0\,,

(4.140)

i.e.
the Hamiltonians associated to the timelike slices are independent of the slice, just as in the one-ended case. As we consider two-ended slices, the Hamiltonians now take the form

\displaystyle H_{\xi}|_{C}

\displaystyle=Q_{\xi}^{+}[C]-Q_{\xi}^{-}[C]\,,

(4.141)

with

Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{\pm}}T^{\pm k}_{\phantom{+}l}\xi_{\pm(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)\pm}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\int_{\partial C^{\pm}}d\sigma_{i}^{\pm}T_{\pm}^{ij}\xi^{\pm}_{j(0)}\,,

(4.142)

where we introduced
d\sigma_{i}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{(0)\pm}_{ijk}dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}
and the derivation of the equation above is given in Appendix
E
. Using (
4.141
), we can express the slice independence property (
4.140
) as

\int_{\partial C_{1}^{+}}d\sigma_{i}^{+}T_{+}^{ij}\xi^{+}_{j(0)}-\int_{\partial C_{2}^{+}}d\sigma^{+}_{i}T_{+}^{ij}\xi^{+}_{j(0)}=\int_{\partial C_{1}^{-}}d\sigma^{-}_{i}T_{-}^{ij}\xi^{-}_{j(0)}-\int_{\partial C_{2}^{-}}d\sigma_{i}^{-}T_{-}^{ij}\xi^{-}_{j(0)}\,,

(4.143)

which tells us that the difference between the charges integrals at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is equal to the difference at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
,

\Delta Q_{\xi}^{+}=\Delta Q_{\xi}^{-}\,,

(4.144)

where
\Delta Q_{\xi}^{\pm}=Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{1}]
. We have thus shown that
\Delta Q_{\xi}
is a conserved quantity under time evolution from
\mathscr{I}^{-}
to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

We can in fact go further than this, and explicitly show that the differences at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
are both identically zero (
i.e.
each side of equation (
4.143
) vanishes identically). The proof is illustrated on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in (
4.120
) and an identical proof follows at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. We thus arrive at the result

Q_{\xi}^{+}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{+}[C_{1}]=Q_{\xi}^{-}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{-}[C_{1}]=0\,,

(4.145)

which tells us that for spacetimes which possess double ACKVs
\xi
, not only is
\left.H_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}}-\left.H_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}}=0
, but the contributions vanish independently at each end.

4.5.2
Interior contribution

We now consider a second type of past boundary, namely that of an interior spacelike hypersurface
I_{t}
, which we do not assume to be in either asymptotic region of the spacetime. We consider the scenario depicted in Figure
7

Figure 7:
Penrose diagram of an AldS spacetime where the timelike hypersurfaces
C
stretch between
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, including an interior hypersurface
I_{t}
.

and we gauge fix the metric in the vicinity of the hypersurface
I_{t}
to be

ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+\gamma^{t}_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}\,,

(4.146)

over which we wish to perform similar integrals of the symplectic current to those considered in the previous subsection. We begin by considering the following integral

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{R_{2}}d\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=\int_{\partial R_{2}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)\,,

(4.147)

where
\tilde{\bm{\omega}}
is defined in (
4.136
) and we again consider
\xi|_{\mathscr{I}^{-}}=\xi^{-}_{(0)}
where
\xi^{-}_{(0)}
is a conformal Killing vector of
g^{-}_{(0)}
. Performing Stokes’ theorem again, we find

-\int_{I_{t}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=\int_{C_{2}^{\text{L}}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)-\int_{C_{1}^{\text{L}}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)\,,

(4.148)

where we have made use of
\tilde{\bm{\omega}}|_{\mathscr{I}^{-}}=0
and
C_{1,2}^{\text{L}}
denote the portions of
C_{1,2}
to the past of
I_{t}
, bounding the region
R_{2}
as shown in Figure
7
. We note that this formula contains an interior contribution from the hypersurface
I_{t}
, which will be our main consideration in this section. As in the previous subsection, we use
\tilde{\bm{\omega}}=d\left[\delta\mathbf{Q}-\bm{\omega}_{\text{ct}}-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}\right]
in order to rewrite the terms on the r.h.s. as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int_{I_{t}}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)&=\int_{C_{2}^{L}\cap I_{t}}(\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta})-\delta\int_{C_{2}^{L}\cap\mathscr{I}^{-}}(\mathbf{Q}-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}-i_{\xi}\mathbf{B})\\
&\phantom{aa}-\int_{C_{1}^{L}\cap I_{t}}(\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta})+\delta\int_{C_{1}^{L}\cap\mathscr{I}^{-}}(\mathbf{Q}-\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}-i_{\xi}\mathbf{B})\\
&=\int_{C_{2}^{L}\cap I_{t}}(\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta})-\int_{C_{1}^{L}\cap I_{t}}(\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta})\,,\end{split}

(4.149)

where we have used the fact that the counterterms and boundary conditions give
\bm{\Theta}|_{\mathscr{I}^{-}}-d\bm{\Theta}_{\text{ct}}|_{\mathscr{I}^{-}}=\delta\mathbf{B}
, and the conservation of the charges under radial translation in
\mathscr{I}^{-}
.
At this point we do not know if
\bm{\Theta}|_{I_{t}}=\delta\mathbf{\tilde{B}}
, and thus we cannot even establish if Hamiltonians associated with
C_{1,2}^{L}
even exist. Checking the existence criterion (
3.17
) and using the formula (
4.48
) for the pullback of the symplectic potential to a spacelike hypersurface, we find the Hamiltonians exist iff

\int_{C\cap I_{t}}i_{\xi}\tilde{\bm{\omega}}(g,\delta_{1}g,\delta_{2}g)=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}i_{\xi}\left[\left(\delta_{1}\pi^{ij}\wedge\delta_{2}\gamma_{ij}\right)d\mu\right]=0\,,

(4.150)

which must be enforced by the behaviour of the bulk fields
at
I_{t}
. We note that we can no longer use the asymptotic expansion of the fields as
I_{t}
is assumed to be a generic hypersurface in the interior of the spacetime. In fact, we are not free to prescribe boundary conditions at
I_{t}
as such conditions should be determined dynamically as a result of the boundary conditions at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
.

As a concrete case we consider
I_{t}
to lie in the future domain of dependence of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. By causality, there can be no flux through
I_{t}
and thus the Hamiltonians associated with
C_{1,2}^{L}
must exist. This means that
105

\bm{\Theta}|_{I_{t}}=\delta\mathbf{\tilde{B}}\,,

(4.151)

where we no longer need to consider the contribution of additional exact terms as in (
4.100
) due to the fact that we are now working in the deep interior of spacetime and thus do not have to add the counterterms in order to deal with the boundary non-normalisable modes. Such a result means that the Hamiltonians take the form

H_{\xi}|_{C^{L}}=Q^{t}_{\xi}[C]-Q_{\xi}^{-}[C]\,,

(4.152)

where

Q^{t}_{\xi}[C]=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\mathbf{\tilde{B}}\,.

(4.153)

We will conclude this subsection by giving two examples of
boundary conditions at
I_{t}
, which respect (
4.151
).

Dirichlet
: Our first example of such boundary conditions are Dirichlet boundary conditions, considered most notably in
38

\delta\gamma_{ij}|_{I_{t}}=0\,,

(4.154)

for which equation (
4.48
) becomes

\bm{\Theta}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\delta(K\sqrt{\gamma})d\mu\implies\mathbf{\tilde{B}}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}K\sqrt{\gamma}d\mu\,.

(4.155)

In order to derive the charge, we note that the Dirichlet boundary conditions are preserved by a bulk vector field
\xi
whose limit to
I_{t}
satisfies

\xi^{t}=0\,,\qquad\xi^{i}=\xi^{i}(x)\,,\qquad D_{(i}\xi_{j)}=0\,,

(4.156)

i.e.
the limit to
I_{t}
is a
Killing vector
of
I_{t}
. Using (
4.153
) and the expression (
4.111
) for the Noether charge 2-form, we can immediately compute the future contribution to
H_{\xi}|_{C^{L}}

Q^{t}_{\xi}[C]=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\mathbf{\tilde{B}}=-\int_{I_{t}\cap C}\pi^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}\varepsilon_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,.

(4.157)

We note that an alternative route to deriving this charge follows from computation of the pullback of
\bm{\omega}
to
I_{t}
(derivation in appendix
F
)

\bm{\omega}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=\left[2\delta\left(D_{i}(\pi^{ij}\xi_{j})\right)-D_{i}\left(\xi^{i}\pi^{jk}\delta\gamma_{jk}\right)\right]d\mu\,,

(4.158)

and using the usual definition of the Hamiltonian (
3.12
) we again reach (
4.157
).

Finally, we also note that the full Hamiltonian given in (
4.152
) is independently radially invariant at each end. The proof at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
is given in (
4.120
) and thus we will only need to check the radial invariance at
I_{t}
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta Q_{\xi}^{t}[C_{2}]-\delta Q_{\xi}^{t}[C_{1}]&=-\int_{I^{12}_{t}}\bm{\omega}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=-\delta\int_{I^{12}_{t}}D_{i}(\pi^{ij}\xi_{j})\,d\mu=0\,,\end{split}

(4.159)

where radial invariance is ensured by the momentum constraint (
4.20
) and the Killing equation (
4.156
).

Neumann
: This is an alternative boundary condition which has been studied in the context of general relativity
e.g.

62
;
116
. In contrast to (
4.154
), this boundary condition is

\delta\pi^{ij}|_{I_{t}}=0\,,

(4.160)

for which equation (
4.48
) becomes
15
15

15

Note that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=0
is equivalent to the statement that the bare Eintein-Hilbert action is sufficient for a well-posed variational problem
i.e.
one does not have to add the Gibbons-Hawking or other boundary terms to the action. This phenomenon was observed in
116
for general relativity with Neumann boundary conditions.

\bm{\Theta}=-\gamma_{ij}\delta\pi^{ij}=0\implies\mathbf{\tilde{B}}=0\,,

(4.161)

and thus the charge is

Q^{t}_{\xi}[C]=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\mathbf{Q}[\xi]=-\int_{I_{t}\cap C}K^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}\varepsilon_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,,

(4.162)

which is also radially invariant using

\delta Q_{\xi}^{t}[C_{2}]-\delta Q_{\xi}^{t}[C_{1}]=-\int_{I^{12}_{t}}\bm{\omega}(g,\delta g,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g)=-\int_{I^{12}_{t}}\left(\delta\pi^{ij}\wedge\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\right)d\mu=0\,,

(4.163)

via the Neumann boundary condition (
4.160
).

We note that the imposition of Dirichlet/Neumann boundary conditions (
4.154
)/(
4.160
) at the interior hypersurface are simply two examples of boundary conditions which define a Hamiltonian. More generally, the boundary conditions on the interior hypersurface which lead to existence of the Hamiltonian
H_{\xi}|_{C}^{L}
will be any that satisfy equation (
4.150
). We note that when equation (
4.150
) is satisfied, in analogy with (
4.144
) we will always have

\Delta Q_{\xi}^{t}=\Delta Q_{\xi}^{-}\,,

(4.164)

where
\Delta Q^{t,-}_{\xi}=Q^{t,-}_{\xi}[C_{2}]-Q^{t,-}_{\xi}[C_{1}]
,
and thus the difference between neighbouring spacelike separated charges is a conserved quantity under time evolution from
\mathscr{I}^{-}
to
I_{t}
. We will see in the next subsection how the violation of the existence property (
4.150
) relates to the presence of gravitational flux through the hypersurface
I_{t}
.

4.6
Past contribution to the fluxes

The two-ended cases discussed thus far have assumed existence of ACKVs, resulting in radial invariance of charges at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. In this section we will relax this assumption, and derive the expressions for the modified Hamiltonians by allowing for fluxes at both components of the conformal boundary as well as an interior surface of the spacetime. The one-ended case of fluxes (usually taken as
\mathscr{I}^{+}
) has previously been studied in works such as
4
;
63
;
59
;
83
;
114
as was outlined in Section
4.4.3
. In this section we extend such results to generic two-ended hypersurfaces. We first discuss the case of flux at both boundaries
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
and then we will discuss the case of flux through an interior hypersurface.

4.6.1
Contribution at
\mathscr{I}^{-}

We begin by considering the setup for the passage of gravitational radiation as depicted in Figure
8
,

Figure 8:
Penrose diagram of an AldS spacetime with flux due to gravitational radiation passing through both boundaries
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
.

which we want to describe using the language of charges and fluxes. In order to do this, we utilise the Hamiltonian modification procedure described in (
3.19
) at both ends of
C
. From the one-ended modification procedure discussed in Section
4.4.3
, we see that the explicit charge expression (
4.131
) is not altered by the modification procedure, but rather the flux formula (
4.132
) is non-zero. Extending this argument to the two-ended case, we find that the modified Hamiltonian associated with a slice is now

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{C}

\displaystyle=Q_{\xi}^{+}[C]-Q_{\xi}^{-}[C]\,,

(4.165)

with

Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{\pm}}T^{\pm k}_{\phantom{+}l}\xi_{\pm(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)\pm}_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\int_{\partial C^{\pm}}d\sigma_{i}^{\pm}\,T_{\pm}^{ij}\xi^{\pm}_{j(0)}\,,

(4.166)

in agreement with equations (
4.141
) and (
4.142
) as the charge expression does not change. As discussed, what changes are the flux formulae, with the difference between two modified Hamiltonians being given by

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{C_{2}}-\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{C_{1}}=Q_{\xi}^{+}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{-}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{+}[C_{1}]+Q_{\xi}^{-}[C_{1}]\,,

(4.167)

where due to the non-zero flux at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
we can no longer use
Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{1}]=0
. Applying the flux formulae (
4.132
) and recalling the definition of the flux form (
3.22
)

Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{2}]-Q_{\xi}^{\pm}[C_{1}]=-\int_{B^{\pm}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{\pm}_{\xi_{(0)}^{\pm}}=\int_{B^{\pm}_{12}}\sqrt{g^{\pm}_{(0)}}T_{\pm}^{ij}\nabla^{\pm}_{(0)(i}\xi^{\pm}_{j)(0)}\,d\mu\,,

(4.168)

we find the overall difference between modified Hamiltonians is

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{C_{2}}-\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{C_{1}}=-\int_{B^{+}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}^{+}}+\int_{B^{-}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{-}_{\xi_{(0)}^{-}}\,,

(4.169)

an equation which captures the effects of gravitational radiation at both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
of an AldS
4
spacetime.

A particularly interesting application of the flux formula (
4.169
) is the case of a closed system where the net flux must be zero. An example of such an AldS
4
spacetime would be one where both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
are Cauchy slices. Due to the zero net flux condition we have

\int_{B^{+}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}^{+}}=\int_{B^{-}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{-}_{\xi_{(0)}^{-}}\iff Q^{+}_{\xi}[C_{2}]-Q^{+}_{\xi}[C_{1}]=Q^{-}_{\xi}[C_{2}]-Q^{-}_{\xi}[C_{1}]\,,

(4.170)

which is an equation which tells us that even though the quantities defined at cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
will change due to the presence of the radiation, the difference between neighbouring charges remains constant. In the absence of any radiation this result reduces to (
4.145
), a stronger condition which also requires the charges on each Cauchy slice to be radially invariant.

4.6.2
Interior contribution

The second important two-ended case occurs when one of the hypersurfaces is in the interior of the spacetime. We will consider the setup depicted in Figure
9

Figure 9:
Penrose diagram of an AldS spacetime with flux due to gravitational radiation passing through the conformal boundary component
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and an interior hypersurface
I_{t}
.

which together with the analysis from Section
4.6.1
will be sufficient to consider all cases (
e.g.

\mathscr{I}^{-}
to an interior hypersurface or two interiors). In order to derive the full expression, we first focus on the case of the interior surface alone as this is the novel part of this section. We recall that on the interior surface Hamilton’s equations read

\delta H^{t}_{\xi}=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}\mathbf{\Theta}\,,

(4.171)

where due to the flux through
I_{t}
we can no longer use equation (
4.151
). We can modify the Hamiltonian on the interior slice by following the prescription (
3.19
), finding

\delta\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\delta\mathbf{Q}-i_{\xi}(\mathbf{\Theta}-\bm{\theta})\,,

(4.172)

where we need to select
\bm{\theta}
using the criterion in (
3.20
). Using (
4.48
), the pullback of the symplectic current to
I_{t}
is given by

\bar{\bm{\omega}}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)=\left(\delta_{1}\pi^{ij}\wedge\delta_{2}\gamma_{ij}\right)d\mu\,,

(4.173)

which allows us to identify that the modification term must be

\bm{\theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)=\left(\pi^{ij}\delta\gamma_{ij}+\delta W(\psi)\right)d\mu\,,

(4.174)

where
W
is an arbitrary local functional of the dynamical field
\gamma_{ij}
on
I_{t}
and represents the ambiguity in this prescription.

Unlike the case of
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
, where it was argued in (
4.130
) that one must set the ambiguity to zero, for an interior surface there is no way to fix the ambiguity. As an example of a choice of
W
, we take

W=0\,,

(4.175)

which makes the interior contribution to the overall Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}=\int_{C\cap I_{t}}\left(\mathbf{Q}-\frac{1}{8\pi G}K\sqrt{\gamma}\,i_{\xi}d\mu\right)=-\int_{I_{t}\cap C}\pi^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}\varepsilon_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,,

(4.176)

a result in agreement with the charge (
4.157
) corresponding to the choice of Dirichlet boundary conditions, precisely the spacelike analogue of the Brown-York quasilocal charge
38
, a natural choice for the contribution to the charge arising at the interior corner. However, due to the flux we no longer have invariance of this quantity, with the flux formula now reading

\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}\cap I_{t}}-\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}\cap I_{t}}=-\int_{I_{t}}\mathbf{F}^{t}_{\xi}=-\int_{I_{t}}\pi^{ij}\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}d\mu=-\int_{I_{t}}\pi^{ij}L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}\,d\mu\,,

(4.177)

where we used the fact that
\xi^{t}=0
in the third equality. This gives a possible modification to the interior contribution in order to define a charge.

For the example above, the full modified Hamiltonian corresponding to the situation depicted in Figure
9
is

\left.\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\right|_{C^{U}}=Q_{\xi}^{+}[C]-Q_{\xi}^{t}[C]\,,

(4.178)

where
Q^{+}_{\xi}[C]
is given in (
4.142
) and
Q^{t}_{\xi}[C]
in (
4.157
). Note that interior contribution to the modified Hamiltonian now comes with the opposite sign to that of the case of no radiation considered in (
4.152
) due to the fact that
I_{t}
lies to the past of
B_{12}^{+}
. The corresponding flux formula for this setup reads

\left.\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\right|_{C^{U}_{2}}-\left.\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\right|_{C^{U}_{1}}=-\int_{B^{+}_{12}}\mathbf{F}^{+}_{\xi_{(0)}^{+}}+\int_{I_{t}}\mathbf{F}^{t}_{\xi}\,,

(4.179)

where the fluxes on the right hand side are defined in equations (
4.168
) and (
4.177
) respectively.

4.6.3
Remarks and future directions

We note that one of the unsatisfying aspects of the interior flux formula is the lack of a unique choice for the modification ambiguity
W
. We previously considered the case of
W=0
but if we leave this arbitrary term in the formulae then we obtain

\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{C\cap I_{t}}W\xi^{i}\varepsilon_{ijk}dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}\,,

(4.180)

for the modified Hamiltonian, and

\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}\cap I_{t}}-\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}\cap I_{t}}\rightarrow\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}\cap I_{t}}-\left.\mathcal{H}^{t}_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}\cap I_{t}}+\int_{I_{t}}L_{\xi}Wd\mu\,,

(4.181)

for the flux. This result somewhat parallels the discussion of Section
4.5.2
, where the lack of uniquely defined boundary conditions at the interior hypersurface meant that one could not uniquely identify the contribution to the Hamiltonian arising from
C\cap I_{t}
. This ambiguity arose from the general existence criterion (
4.151
) and can also be described by the ambuguity
W
, even though in that case there is no flux!

In order to demonstrate this, we show that a choice of
W\neq 0
can transform between the Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions as discussed in Section
4.5.2
. Starting with the generic expression for the pullback of the symplectic potential as given in (
4.48
), we have

\bm{\Theta}-\bm{\theta}=\delta\left(\frac{K\sqrt{\gamma}}{8\pi G}-W\right)d\mu\,,

(4.182)

which is a total variation and thus will always lead to a well defined modified Hamiltonian. We note choosing
W=0
recovers the Dirichlet charge (
4.157
) and
W=(K\sqrt{\gamma})/(8\pi G)
the Neumann charge (
4.162
), both of which are radially invariant when the respective boundary conditions are imposed at
I_{t}
. We see that the ambiguity is still undetermined even in the absence of flux and it is thus not possible for us to fix the ambiguity when flux is present. This issue is part of the wider question of attempting to define a quasi-local mass in general relativity
167
, as there is no well-defined notion of local energy density (due to the equivalence principle).

We conclude this section with a comment on a possible way in which one could begin to identify
W
. This approach would be to impose some additional conditions on the spacetime which restrict the passage of gravitational radiation. A particularly interesting condition is that of the ‘no-incoming radiation’ condition of
13
, which mimics physical systems such as compact binaries by imposing that no radiation passes through the null surface
\mathscr{I}_{\text{rel}}
, the future event horizon of past timelike infinity
i^{-}
16
16

16

i^{\pm}
are the endpoints of the worldline of the radiation source
. If such a condition is imposed in our formalism, then the past event horizon of future timelike infinity
i^{+}
will have the same flux as
\mathscr{I}^{+}
by causality, and thus one can partially fix
W
by demanding that the flux through that null hypersurface is equivalent to the well-defined flux through
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Realising this construction via our formalism will require an extension to describe null hypersurfaces in the bulk, as the current technology merely describes spacelike slices
I_{t}
.

5
Examples of spacetimes with radially invariant gravitational charges

5.1
dS
4

A warm-up in studying the charges of the more complicated solutions is to study pure
dS_{4}
. As we shall see, this solutions does not possess non-trivial conserved charges, but will serve as an illustration of the techniques that one needs to employ in order to compute the charges of other AldS
4
spacetimes.

We start by presenting the dS
4
solution in global coordiantes

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\cos^{2}\eta}(-d\eta^{2}+d\Omega_{3}^{2})\,,

(5.1)

where
\eta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
is the conformal time coordinate and
d\Omega_{3}^{2}
is the metric of the unit round
S^{3}
. In these coordinates,
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
are located at
\eta=\pm\pi/2
.

In order to extract the conserved charges of this solution, we perform the transformation from global coordinates to Starobinsky gauge at both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. In order to do this one merely needs to solve

\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\cos^{2}\eta}=\frac{d\rho_{\pm}^{2}}{\rho_{\pm}^{2}}\implies\frac{d\eta}{\cos\eta}=\mp\frac{d\rho_{\pm}}{\rho_{\pm}}\,,

(5.2)

where the signs in the equation above are chosen to account for the ranges of the coordinates, recall
\rho_{+}<0
and
\rho_{-}>0
. Solving this equation gives

\rho_{\pm}=\mp 2\frac{\cos\left(\frac{\eta}{2}\right)\mp\sin\left(\frac{\eta}{2}\right)}{\cos\left(\frac{\eta}{2}\right)\pm\sin\left(\frac{\eta}{2}\right)}\,,

(5.3)

where the factor of 2 is a choice of integration constant. Using this map we find that the metric (
5.1
) takes the form

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{\pm}^{2}}\left(-d\rho_{\pm}^{2}+\frac{1}{\cos^{2}(\eta(\rho_{\pm}))}d\Omega^{2}_{3}\right)\,,

(5.4)

which upon expanding
\cos^{2}\eta
near the components of the conformal boundary gives near
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}

\left.ds^{2}\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{\pm}}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{\pm}^{2}}\left[-d\rho_{\pm}^{2}+\left(1+\rho_{\pm}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{\pm}^{4})\right)d\Omega_{3}^{2}\right]\,,

(5.5)

where we note that in particular
g^{(3)}_{\pm}=0
and thus all charges vanish at both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. An important note about the Starobinsky coordinates used to cover the neighbourhood of each boundary is that they are directly related via an inverse relation. Using (
5.3
) we find immediately

\rho_{+}=-\frac{4}{\rho_{-}}\,,

(5.6)

and thus we see that
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is located at
\rho_{-}=\infty
and similarly
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is at
\rho_{+}=-\infty
. From the perspective of analytic continuation, either of these can be obtained from the continuation of a single copy of
AdS_{4}
using the rules in equation (
2.19
) although we note that due to the relationship (
5.6
) above, this analytic continuation is valid for only one of the boundaries, one cannot analytically continue
AdS_{4}
to obtain both boundaries simultaneously (as expected, since the global structure of
dS_{4}
is different from that of
AdS_{4}
).

5.2
Schwarzchild-dS
4

In this subsection we will compute all of the charges of the Schwarzchild-dS
4
(SdS) solution, whose metric is given by

ds^{2}=-h(r)dt^{2}+\frac{1}{h(r)}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,\qquad h(r)=1-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}-\frac{2m}{r}\,.

(5.7)

In order for
h(r)
to possess two positive roots, we require

m<\frac{\ell}{3\sqrt{3}}\,,

(5.8)

i.e.
the mass parameter is below the Nariai bound. We denote the two roots as
h(r_{h})=h(r_{c})=0
where
r_{h}<r_{c}
. The smaller root corresponds to the black hole horizon and the larger the cosmological horizon. As we are interested in the conformal boundary of the solution, we first restrict consideration to
r\in(r_{c},\infty)
, as
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is located at
r=\infty
.

In order to compute the charges of this solution, we first map this metric into the Starobinsky gauge
153
up to the required order. To do this, we follow a similar route to the computation of
136
which maps SAdS into Fefferman-Graham coordinates
74
, namely we first perform the map inverting the radial coordinate

r=-\frac{\ell^{2}}{z}\,,

(5.9)

then expand
z
in the following power series in
\rho_{+}

z=\rho_{+}-\frac{\rho_{+}^{3}}{4\ell^{2}}-\frac{m\rho_{+}^{4}}{3\ell^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{5})\,,

(5.10)

which is sufficient to read off all of the useful information in the Starobinsky-SdS expansion. As a final step, we work with dimensionless coordinates

\tilde{\rho}_{+}=\frac{\rho_{+}}{\ell}\,,\qquad\tilde{t}=\frac{t}{\ell}\,,

(5.11)

but we will drop the tildes from here on. The steps above produce a Starobinsky expansion of the following form

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{+}^{2}}\left(-d\rho_{+}^{2}+g_{(0)}^{+}+\rho_{+}^{2}g_{(2)}^{+}+\rho_{+}^{3}g_{(3)}^{+}+\ldots\right)\,,

(5.12)

where

\displaystyle g_{(0)}^{+}

\displaystyle=dt^{2}+d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,

(5.13)

\displaystyle g_{(2)}^{+}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,,

(5.14)

\displaystyle g_{(3)}^{+}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2m}{3\ell}(2dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,.

(5.15)

This expansion allows us to construct the holographic energy-momentum tensor

T^{+}=-\frac{3\ell}{16\pi G}g^{(3)}=\frac{2m}{16\pi G}(2dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,,

(5.16)

and the charges associated to this solution are thus

Q^{+}_{\xi}=\oint_{\partial C}T^{+}_{ij}n^{i}\xi^{j}\sqrt{g_{\Omega_{2}}}\,d^{2}x=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}4m\xi^{t}(t,\theta,\phi)\sin\theta\,d\phi d\theta\,,

(5.17)

where we used
n^{i}=(\frac{\rho_{+}^{2}}{\ell^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{4}))(\partial_{t})^{i}
,
\sqrt{g_{\Omega_{2}}}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{+}^{2}}\sin\theta+\mathcal{O}(1)
and all higher order terms vanish in the limit as
