\rho_{+}\rightarrow 0
(one can also work directly on the
\rho_{+}=0
hypersurface in the unphysical spacetime). The key point to note in the above formula is that all of the charges for the SdS solution only depend upon the
t
-component of the vector field
\xi
, and thus the computation boils down to computing
\xi^{t}
for the conformal Killing vectors of (
5.13
).

In order to compute these vectors, we use the fact that the SdS metric is asymptotically dS,
i.e.
it has a conformally flat
g_{(0)}
. To see this, we start with the metric in (
5.13
) and perform the following coordinate transformation

x=\frac{\sinh t}{\cos\theta+\cosh t}\,,\qquad y=\frac{\cos\phi\sin\theta}{\cos\theta+\cosh t}\,,\qquad z=\frac{\sin\phi\sin\theta}{\cos\theta+\cosh t}\,,

(5.18)

which brings the line element into the form

g^{(0)}=\Omega^{2}(dx^{2}+dy^{2}+dz^{2})\,,\qquad\Omega=\cosh t+\cos\theta\,,

(5.19)

demonstrating that this metric is conformally equivalent to the standard flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Recall that conformally equivalent metrics share the same conformal Killing vectors
17
17

17

To see this, note that if
\xi
is a CKV of a metric
g
,

{\cal L}_{\xi}g=2\phi g
, then it is also a CKV of the metric
g^{\prime}=\Omega g
,
{\cal L}_{\xi}g^{\prime}=2\psi g^{\prime}
, with
\psi=\phi+{\cal L}_{\xi}\log\Omega
.
. Thus the CKV of
g^{(0)}
is that of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The set of conformal Killing vectors on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
are well-known, see
e.g.

87
;
70
and separate into the usual translations

\xi^{i}_{T}=a^{i}\,,

(5.20)

rotations

\xi^{i}_{R}=r^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}x^{j}\,,\qquad(r_{ij}=-r_{ji})\,,

(5.21)

dilatation

\xi^{i}_{D}=\lambda x^{i}\,,

(5.22)

and special conformal transformations (SCTs)

\xi^{i}_{SCT}=b^{i}x_{j}x^{j}-2x^{i}b_{j}x^{j}\,,

(5.23)

where
a^{i},r_{ij},\lambda,b^{i}
are all spacetime constants. Counting degrees of freedom we have
3+3+1+3=10
CKVs for which we would like to compute the corresponding charges. The first step is to compute the
t
-components of all of the CKVs under the coordinate transformation (
5.18
), for which the following derivatives are useful

\frac{\partial t}{\partial x}=1+\cos\theta\cosh t\,,\qquad\frac{\partial t}{\partial y}=-\cos\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,,\qquad\frac{\partial t}{\partial z}=-\sin\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,,

(5.24)

allowing us to compute

\displaystyle(\partial_{x})^{t}=1+\cos\theta\cosh t\,,

(5.25)

\displaystyle(\partial_{y})^{t}=-\cos\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,

(5.26)

\displaystyle(\partial_{z})^{t}=-\sin\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,,

(5.27)

\displaystyle(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})^{t}=-\cos\phi\sin\theta\cosh t\,,

(5.28)

\displaystyle(x\partial_{z}-z\partial_{x})^{t}=-\sin\phi\sin\theta\cosh t\,,

(5.29)

\displaystyle(y\partial_{z}-z\partial_{y})^{t}=0\,,

(5.30)

\displaystyle(x\partial_{x}+y\partial_{y}+z\partial_{z})^{t}=(\cos\theta\sinh t)\,,

(5.31)

\displaystyle([-x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}]\partial_{x}-2xy\partial_{y}-2xz\partial_{z})^{t}=1-\cos\theta\cosh t\,,

(5.32)

\displaystyle([-y^{2}+x^{2}+z^{2}]\partial_{x}-2xy\partial_{x}-2yz\partial_{z})^{t}=-\cos\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,,

(5.33)

\displaystyle([-x^{2}+x^{2}+y^{2}]\partial_{x}-2xz\partial_{x}-2yz\partial_{y})^{t}=-\sin\phi\sin\theta\sinh t\,.

(5.34)

As (
5.17
) involves an integral over
S^{2}
, the only CKV that can lead to a non-zero charge are the ones which contain parts that are independent of the angles and these are the vectors given in (
5.25
) and (
5.32
). The charges corresponding to these two vectors are

\displaystyle Q_{\partial_{x}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}4m(1+\cos\theta\cosh t)\sin\theta\,d\phi d\theta=\frac{m}{G}\,,

(5.35)

\displaystyle Q_{(-x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})\partial_{x}-2xy\partial_{y}-2xz\partial_{z}}=\frac{m}{G}\,,

(5.36)

i.e.
the
x
-translation and the
x
-SCT give non-vanishing and equivalent charges. This is reminiscent of that encountered in
136
where the “mass aspect” for an AdS black brane solution was found to be a linear combination of the time translation and SCT aspects. Indeed, the same result holds in the dS case, which we can observe simply by noting

\frac{1}{2}\left[(\partial_{x})^{t}+([-x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}]\partial_{x}-2xy\partial_{y}-2xz\partial_{z})^{t}\right]=1\,,

(5.37)

which reproduces precisely the Schwarzchild mass aspect in the integrand of (
5.17
).

5.2.1
SdS charges at
\mathscr{I}^{-}

The previous section analyses the charges of the SdS solution near
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We note that one can use the Starobinsky expansion from the original coordinates (
5.7
) in order to cover
\mathscr{I}^{-}
in much the same way one does
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, although now the direction of the “time” along the boundary
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is globally opposite to that at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In order to demonstrate this carefully, we will now discuss the “Kruskalisation”
26
;
29
of the SdS solution in order to obtain coordinates regular across both the past and future cosmological horizons
\mathscr{H}^{-}_{\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathscr{H}^{+}_{\mathscr{C}}
respectively.

First we transform to coordinates which are smooth across the past cosmological horizon
\mathscr{H}^{-}_{\mathscr{C}}
. In order to find such coordinates, one first defines the tortoise coordinate in the usual manner

r^{*}=\int\frac{dr}{h(r)}\,,

(5.38)

and then introduces the
advanced
time coordinate via

v=t+r_{*}\,,

(5.39)

bringing the metric into the form

ds^{2}=-h(r)dv^{2}+2dvdr+r^{2}d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,

(5.40)

where
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is now given by
r=\infty
and the coordinate
v
is finite along
\mathscr{I}^{-}
.

In order to understand more intricately how the two-ended charge computation will work for SdS, we will transform the metric (
5.40
) into a form which is regular across both the past and future cosmological horizons
\mathscr{H}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{C}}
, following the original paper
26
which performs this calculation in detail. We begin by noting
26
;
29
that

h(r)=\frac{(r-r_{h})(r_{c}-r)(r+r_{h}+r_{c})}{r(r_{h}^{2}+r_{h}r_{c}+r_{c}^{2})}\,,

(5.41)

where
h(r_{h})=h(r_{c})=h(-r_{h}-r_{c})=0
, and
r_{h}<r_{c}
, with
r_{h}
the black hole horizon and
r_{c}
the cosmological horizon. We also note that the explicit form of the tortoise coordinate (
5.38
) is given by
29

r^{*}=\delta_{h}\log\frac{|r-r_{h}|}{r+r_{h}+r_{c}}-\delta_{c}\log\frac{|r_{c}-r|}{r+r_{h}+r_{c}}+\delta_{h}\left[\log\left(\frac{r_{c}}{r_{h}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\right]\,,

(5.42)

where

\delta_{h}=\frac{r_{h}}{1-\Lambda r_{h}^{2}}>0\,,\qquad\delta_{c}=-\frac{r_{c}}{1-\Lambda r_{c}^{2}}>0\,.

(5.43)

The positivity of
\delta_{h}
and
\delta_{c}
is a direct result of
\left.(rh)^{\prime}\right|_{r_{h}}>0
and
\left.(rh)^{\prime}\right|_{r_{c}}<0
, respectively.
The constant term in (
5.42
) matches that of
29
, where it was chosen in order to recover a sensible flat limit (
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
) from the SdS solution. We recall the definition of the
retarded
time coordinate

u=t-r^{*}\,,

(5.44)

which can be used to analyse
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as
u
is finite along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In order to cover both boundaries simultaneously we introduce the coordinates

U=\left(\frac{r_{c}}{r_{h}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{h}}{2\delta_{c}}}\exp\left(\frac{u-\delta_{h}}{2\delta_{c}}\right)\,,\quad V=-\left(\frac{r_{c}}{r_{h}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{h}}{2\delta_{c}}}\exp\left(-\frac{v+\delta_{h}}{2\delta_{c}}\right)\,,

(5.45)

in which the metric (
5.40
) becomes

ds^{2}=-\frac{4\Lambda\delta_{c}^{2}}{3r}(r-r_{h})^{1+\frac{\delta_{h}}{\delta_{c}}}(r+r_{h}+r_{c})^{2-\frac{\delta_{h}}{\delta_{c}}}dUdV+r^{2}d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,

(5.46)

and the location of
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
is now

UV=1\,,

(5.47)

with
\mathscr{I}^{+}
being
U>0,V>0
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}
being
U<0,V<0
. In order to compare these coordinates with those used in equation (37) of
26
, we note that

\displaystyle\tilde{u}+\tilde{v}=V\,,\qquad\tilde{u}-\tilde{v}=-U\,.

(5.48)

These coordinates cover both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
so they should be suitable in analysing a 2-ended hypersurface stretching between the surfaces.

We will now consider the correct vector field to use in order to compute the charges. We know that the correct vector field at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
which we should use to compute the charges is
\partial_{t}
, so all we need to do is identify
\partial_{t}
in coordinates
(U,V)
at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and then we will have a ‘‘global”
18
18

18

Due to the presence of event and cosmological horizons, the SdS solution does not admit global coordinates, see
26
. Here by “global” we mean coordinates which extend over the shaded region of Figure
10
.
vector field with which to compute the charges. We use the map (39) in
26
and obtain

t=-2\delta_{c}\text{arctanh}\left(\frac{\tilde{v}}{\tilde{u}}\right)=-2\delta_{c}\text{arctanh}\left(\frac{V+U}{V-U}\right)\,,

(5.49)

and so

\partial_{t}=\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}\partial_{V}+\frac{\partial U}{\partial t}\partial_{U}=\frac{1}{2\delta_{c}}\left(U\partial_{U}-V\partial_{V}\right)\,,

(5.50)

where we note that
\partial_{t}
is also the correct vector field to compute the charges at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. As explained in
26
, the necessary map between
\mathscr{I}^{+}
to
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is
(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})\rightarrow-(\tilde{u},\tilde{v})

under which (
5.49
) remains invariant.

Figure 10:
Penrose diagram for the SdS solution with the shaded region being that covered by the Kruskal-type coordinates
(U,V)
. The red timelike hypersurface C is used to compute our two-ended Hamiltonian. We indicate the direction of increasing
t
at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
with blue arrows and the signs of the coordinates
(U,V)
which change when one crosses the cosmological horizon. We have also inlcuded the location of the black hole singularity at
r=0
, which lies outside our region of interest. The maximally extended SdS Penrose diagram contains an infinite sequence of singularities and spacelike infinities
85
, of which we show one copy above. An alternative completion of the spacetime is given by identifying the vertical dotted lines on the left and right to wrap once around the Einstein static universe
26
.

We can thus compute the contribution to the two-ended “mass” charge coming from the past end of the timelike slice. All we need is to perform the Starobinsky expansion in a neighbourhood of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
and evaluate the charge associated with
\xi=\partial_{t}
. Starting with (
5.7
) and setting
\ell=1
, we begin with the map

r=\frac{1}{z_{-}}\,,

(5.51)

then perform the expansion in small
\rho_{-}
of the form

z_{-}=\rho_{-}-\frac{1}{4}\rho_{-}^{3}+\frac{m}{3}\rho_{-}^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{-}^{5})\,,

(5.52)

which gives a Starobinsky expansion of the form

ds^{2}=-\frac{d\rho_{-}^{2}}{\rho_{-}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{-}^{2}}\left(g^{-}_{(0)}+\rho_{-}^{2}g^{-}_{(2)}+\rho_{-}^{3}g_{(3)}^{-}+\ldots\right)\,,

(5.53)

with

\displaystyle g_{-}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=dt^{2}+d\Omega_{2}^{2}\,,

(5.54)

\displaystyle g_{-}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,,

(5.55)

\displaystyle g_{-}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m}{3}(2dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,.

(5.56)

Using this expansion, we can obtain the energy-momentum tensor at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
via (
4.42
)

\langle T\rangle^{\mathscr{I}^{-}}=-\frac{3}{16\pi G}g^{-}_{(3)}=-\frac{m}{8\pi G}(2dt^{2}-d\Omega_{2}^{2})\,,

(5.57)

and thus via (
4.142
) we have

Q^{-}_{\xi}[C]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{-k}_{\phantom{+}l}\xi_{-(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)-}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{-t}_{\phantom{-}t}\epsilon^{(0)-}_{tij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=-\frac{m}{G}\,,

(5.58)

which contributes to the total two-ended Hamiltonian via (
4.141
)
i.e.

H_{\xi}|_{C}=Q_{\xi}^{+}-Q_{\xi}^{-}=2\frac{m}{G}\,,

(5.59)

i.e.
including the contribution from the past end, one finds a doubling of the charge associated with the Killing vector given in equation (
5.50
).

In order to understand the physical picture of this result, it is helpful to note the direction of increasing
t
at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
(marked with the blue arrows in Figure
10
) where we note that the directions are “opposite” from the perspective of the Penrose diagram. One can see these directions by direct inspection of (
5.50
), noting that
\partial_{t}
is tangential to
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. In order to see this latter result, note that a normal vector to the
UV=1
surface is given by

n_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}(UV)=V\delta^{U}_{\mu}+U\delta^{V}_{\mu}\,,

(5.60)

and thus

(\partial_{t})^{\mu}n_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\delta_{c}}(UV-VU)=0\,,

(5.61)

justifying the blue arrows shown in Figure
10
. This helps with the interpretation of (
5.59
) in being a “doubling” of the one-ended Hamiltonian, where now the evolution is taken in opposite directions at each end.

5.3
Kerr-dS
4

Another asymptotically dS
4
solution of natural interest is the Kerr-dS
4
(KdS) solution. The metric for the KdS solution can be written in Boyer-Lindquist-type coordinates as
44
;
135
;
85
;
89

\begin{split}ds^{2}=-\frac{\Delta_{r}}{\Xi^{2}\varrho^{2}}&\left(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\,d\phi^{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}dr^{2}+\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}d\theta^{2}\\
&\phantom{\qquad\quad}+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}\varrho^{2}}\left(a\,dt-(r^{2}+a^{2})\,d\phi\right)^{2}\,,\end{split}

(5.62)

where

\displaystyle\varrho

\displaystyle=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,,

(5.63)

\displaystyle\Delta_{r}

\displaystyle=(r^{2}+a^{2})\left(1-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\right)-2mr=(r^{2}+a^{2})\left(1-\frac{r^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)-2mr\,,

(5.64)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\theta}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\Lambda}{3}a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta=1+\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta\,,

(5.65)

\displaystyle\Xi

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\Lambda}{3}a^{2}=1+\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\,.

(5.66)

The spacetime has horizons located at the roots

\Delta_{r}=0\,,

(5.67)

a quartic polynomial which has (up to) four real roots. We will be interested in the case when four real roots are admitted, with the heirarchy of roots being

r_{c_{1}}<0<r_{h_{1}}<r_{h_{2}}<r_{c_{2}}\,,

(5.68)

where
r_{c_{1,2}}
denote the outer and inner cosmological horizons and
r_{h_{1,2}}
the outer and inner event horizons. For more information on the conformal diagrams of such a spacetime see
85
;
89
.

We want to compute the charges of such spacetimes using the formalism developed in Section
4
. In particular, we will be interested in the scenario depicted in Figure
11

Figure 11:
Penrose diagram depicting the region of interest in the KdS solution in order to compute the charges at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. The dotted lines indicate regions of the spacetime which will not be important for our calculation. See
e.g.

85
;
89
;
3
for the full analytic extension of the KdS spacetime.

which will be sufficient to compute the charges of the KdS spacetime at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
. We will first focus on the one-ended case at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, before computing the contribution at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
using the methods applied to the SdS solution in Section
5.2.1
. We will see that the contribution from the past end results in a doubling of the one-ended Hamiltonian as in (
5.59
).

Charges of Kerr-dS:

In order to extract the charges of KdS, we first want to transform coordinates from Boyer-Lindquist coordinates (
5.62
) into Starobinsky gauge (
2.11
) and read off the values of both the representative of the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
,
g^{(0)}_{ij}
, as well as the energy-momentum tensor,
g^{(3)}_{ij}
. The specific steps of the derivation follow closely to the Kerr-AdS (KAdS) case considered in Section 6.1 of
127
, and as such the first step is to perform a simple rescaling of the time coordinate

\tilde{t}=\frac{t}{\Xi}\,,

(5.69)

and then writing (
5.62
) and dropping the tilde, we obtain

ds^{2}=\varrho^{2}\left[\frac{dr^{2}}{\Delta_{r}}+\frac{d\theta^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}\right]+\frac{\Delta_{\theta}\sin^{2}\theta}{\varrho^{2}}\left[adt-(r^{2}+a^{2})\frac{d\phi}{\Xi}\right]^{2}-\frac{\Delta_{r}}{\varrho^{2}}\left[dt-a\sin^{2}\theta\frac{d\phi}{\Xi}\right]^{2}\,.

(5.70)

In order to make sure that the metric we consider is just the analytically continued version of the KAdS metric of
127
under
\ell^{2}\rightarrow-\ell^{2}
. We first expand the metric (
5.70
) for large
r
and find

\displaystyle\begin{split}ds^{2}&=\frac{r^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{r^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right)\right]\left(dt-\frac{a\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi}d\phi\right)^{2}\\
&\quad-\frac{\ell^{2}}{r^{2}}\left[1+\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{r^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right)\right]dr^{2}\\
&\quad+\frac{r^{2}}{\Delta_{\theta}}\left(1+\frac{a^{2}}{r^{2}}\cos^{2}\theta\right)d\theta^{2}\\
&\quad+\frac{r^{2}\Delta_{\theta}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Xi^{2}}\left[d\phi^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{r^{2}}\left((1+\sin^{2}\theta)d\phi^{2}-\frac{2\Xi}{a}d\phi dt\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{r^{4}}\right)\right]\,.\end{split}

(5.71)

This expression is clearly not in the Starobinsky form and will require some extra work to be put in that gauge due to the
\theta
-dependence of the component
g_{rr}
. We introduce new coordinates

\displaystyle\begin{split}r&=\bar{r}-\frac{1}{\bar{r}}f(\bar{\theta})+\mathcal{O}(\bar{r}^{-3})\,,\\
\theta&=\bar{\theta}-\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}h(\bar{\theta})+\mathcal{O}(\bar{r}^{-6})\,,\end{split}

(5.72)

and forcing the
d\bar{r}d\bar{\theta}
cross-terms to vanish as well as the
\theta
-independence of
g_{rr}
fixes

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\bar{\theta})&=-\frac{a^{2}}{4}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}+f_{0}\,,\\
g(\bar{\theta})&=-\frac{1}{8}\ell^{2}a^{2}\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}\sin\bar{\theta}\cos\bar{\theta}\,,\end{split}

(5.73)

where we follow
127
in setting the constant of integration
f_{0}=0
.
These transformations result in a large
\bar{r}
expansion of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}ds^{2}=&-\frac{\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}\left[1+\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}-\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]d\bar{r}^{2}\\
&+\frac{\bar{r}^{2}}{\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}}\left[1+\frac{3a^{2}}{2\bar{r}^{2}}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]d\bar{\theta}^{2}\\
&+\frac{\bar{r}^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}+\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\left(dt-\frac{a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi}d\phi\right)^{2}\\
&+\frac{\bar{r}^{2}\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi^{2}}\left[d\phi^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}\left(\left(2-\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\right)d\phi^{2}-\frac{2\Xi}{a}d\phi dt\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\,,\end{split}

(5.74)

which is almost in the desired form of (
2.11
), but needs a little work in order to fix the “time-time” (here
d\bar{r}^{2}
as
\mathscr{I}^{+}
lies outside the cosmological horizon) component of the metric. The components of the induced metric are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{\bar{\theta}\bar{\theta}}&=\frac{\bar{r}^{2}}{\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}}\left[1+\frac{3a^{2}}{2\bar{r}^{2}}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\,,\\
\gamma_{tt}&=\frac{\bar{r}^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}+\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\,,\\
\gamma_{t\phi}&=-\frac{\bar{r}^{2}a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\ell^{2}\Xi}\left[1+\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\right)\frac{a^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}+\frac{2m\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\,,\\
\gamma_{\phi\phi}&=\frac{\bar{r}^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi}\left[1+\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\right)\frac{a^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}+\frac{2ma^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\bar{r}^{3}\Xi}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\,,\end{split}

(5.75)

which we will use to read off the Starobinsky data after transforming the
d\bar{r}^{2}
component. In order to do this, we first define the canonical time coordinate
\tau
via

d\tau=\ell\left[1+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{a^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\frac{\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{2}}-\frac{m\ell^{2}}{\bar{r}^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\bar{r}^{4}}\right)\right]\frac{d\bar{r}}{\bar{r}}\,,

(5.76)

which brings the metric into the form of (
4.9
)
and finally the (dimensionful) Starobinsky time coordinate
19
19

19

This coordinate is defined such that
\rho_{+}
increases as one moves towards
\mathscr{I}^{+}=\{\rho_{+}=0\}
. This means that
\rho_{+}<0
.
as

\rho_{+}=-\ell\exp\left(-\frac{\tau}{\ell}\right)\,,

(5.77)

for which we can combine the above two transformations in order to write

\bar{r}=-\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{+}}-\frac{\rho_{+}}{4\ell^{2}}(\ell^{2}-a^{2})-\frac{m\rho_{+}^{2}}{3\ell^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{3})\,.

(5.78)

These transformations bring the metric into the desired form of

ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{+}^{2}}\left[d\rho_{+}^{2}+\left(g^{(0)}_{ij}+\rho_{+}^{2}g^{(2)}_{ij}+\rho_{+}^{3}g^{(3)}_{ij}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{+}^{4})\right)dx^{i}dx^{j}\right]\,,

(5.79)

where all components
g_{ij}^{(1,2,3)}
can be read off directly by applying the mapping (
5.78
) to the induced metric components given in (
5.75
). In particular, we find
g^{(0)}_{ij},g^{(3)}_{ij}
(written in line-element form) as

ds_{(0)}^{2}=dt^{2}-\frac{2a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi}dtd\phi+\frac{\ell^{2}}{\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}}d\bar{\theta}^{2}+\frac{\ell^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi}d\phi^{2}\,,

(5.80)

for the representative of the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, and

ds_{(3)}^{2}=-\frac{4m}{3\ell^{4}}dt^{2}+\frac{8ma\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{3\ell^{4}\Xi}dtd\phi+\frac{2m}{3\ell^{2}\Delta_{\bar{\theta}}}d\bar{\theta}^{2}+\frac{2m\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{3\ell^{2}\Xi^{2}}\left(\Xi-3\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\ell^{2}}\right)d\phi^{2}\,,

(5.81)

for the energy-momentum tensor.
We note that one can directly compare our analysis with the KAdS case of
127
via the simple analytic continuation
\ell^{2}\rightarrow-\ell^{2}
as discussed in Section
2.2
. Our convention for the energy-momentum tensor is

T_{ij}=-\frac{3\ell^{2}}{2\kappa^{2}}g_{ij}^{(3)}\,,

(5.82)

which has picked up an additional factor of
\ell
relative to (
4.41
) due to the dimensionful coordinate
\rho_{+}
. The non-zero components of this tensor are

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{t}^{t}=\frac{2m}{\ell^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,,\qquad T_{\bar{\theta}}^{\bar{\theta}}=T_{\phi}^{\phi}=-\frac{m}{\ell^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,,\qquad T_{\phi}^{t}=-\frac{3am\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\ell^{2}\Xi\kappa^{2}}\,,\end{split}

(5.83)

which is a convenient form in order to compute the charges. Recall that charges corresponding to an asymptotic conformal Killing vector,
\xi
, are given in equation (
4.119
) and thus for the KdS solution we have

H_{\xi}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sqrt{g_{(0)}}\,T_{i}^{t}\xi_{(0)}^{i}\,d\bar{\theta}\,d\phi\,,

(5.84)

which we want to evaluate for charges corresponding to the mass and angular momentum.

Angular momentum:

Using the Killing vector
\xi=-\partial_{\phi}
we obtain

\displaystyle J_{+}=H_{-\partial_{\phi}}

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{3am\sin^{3}\bar{\theta}}{\Xi^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,d\bar{\theta}=\frac{8\pi am}{\Xi^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,.

(5.85)

Mass:

This charge requires a slight redefinition of coordinates in order to identify the correct Killing vector, as one can immediately notice from equation (
5.80
) that the representative of the conformal class is not the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{2}
. In order to show that the standard metric is in fact in the conformal class, we consider the following coordinate transformation from
(t,\phi,\bar{\theta})
to
(t^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},\bar{\theta}^{\prime})
:

t^{\prime}=t\,,\qquad\phi^{\prime}=\phi-\frac{a}{\ell^{2}}t\,,\qquad\Xi\tan^{2}\bar{\theta}^{\prime}=\tan^{2}\bar{\theta}\,,

(5.86)

which brings the metric (
5.80
) into the form

ds_{(0)}^{2}=\frac{\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}^{\prime}}\left(dt^{\prime 2}+\ell^{2}d\Omega_{2}^{\prime 2}\right)\,,

(5.87)

which is clearly conformal to the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{2}
. We will thus use the Killing vector
\partial_{t^{\prime}}
in order to define the mass, which can be computed in terms of the old coordinates using the chain rule:

\partial_{t^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial t}{\partial t^{\prime}}\partial_{t}+\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t^{\prime}}\partial_{\phi}=\partial_{t}+\frac{a}{\ell^{2}}\partial_{\phi}\,.

(5.88)

Now applying this to the charge formula we find the following expression for the mass

\displaystyle\begin{split}M_{+}=H_{\partial_{t}+\frac{a}{\ell^{2}}\partial_{\phi}}=-2\pi\int_{0}^{\pi}\left(-\frac{2m\sin\bar{\theta}}{\kappa^{2}\Xi}+\frac{3a^{2}m\sin^{3}\bar{\theta}}{\ell^{2}\Xi^{2}\kappa^{2}}\right)d\bar{\theta}=\frac{8m\pi}{\kappa^{2}\Xi^{2}}\,.\end{split}

(5.89)

Finally we note that this pair is sufficient to compute the charges for the SdS (
a=0
) and dS (
a=m=0
) solutions, namely

(M_{\text{SdS}},J_{\text{SdS}})=\left(\frac{8m\pi}{\kappa^{2}},0\right)\,,\qquad(M_{\text{dS}},J_{\text{dS}})=(0,0)\,,

(5.90)

in agreement with (
5.59
) as
\kappa^{2}=8\pi G
.

5.3.1
KdS charges at
\mathscr{I}^{-}

We have entirely focused on charges at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in the preceding analysis. As in the SdS case disucssed in Section
5.2.1
, the contributions to the total Hamiltonians arising from
\mathscr{I}^{-}
are straightforward to calculate. The computation of these charges can be performed using the “Kruskalisation” of the KdS solution in order to develop a coordinate chart which covers both
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
(as we did for SdS), see
e.g.

95
;
3
;
89
;
52
;
36
for more information on such a procedure. Alternatively, one can directly evaluate the Starobinsky expansion at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, which is what we shall do.

The steps in performing the Starobinksy expansion at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
are similar to those of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as discussed in the previous section, however we now replace the large
\bar{r}
expansion (
5.78
) with

\bar{r}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{\rho_{-}}+\frac{\rho_{-}}{4\ell^{2}}(\ell^{2}-a^{2})-\frac{m\rho_{-}^{2}}{3\ell^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\rho_{-}^{3})\,,

(5.91)

and thus

g_{(3)ij}^{-}=-g_{(3)ij}^{+}\,,

(5.92)

which upon using (
4.42
) gives us

\langle T_{ij}\rangle^{\mathscr{I}^{-}}=-\frac{3\ell^{2}}{16\pi G}g^{-}_{(3)ij}=-\langle T_{ij}\rangle^{\mathscr{I}^{+}}\,,

(5.93)

and so for the angular momentum charge at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{-}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{-k}_{\phantom{--}l}(\partial_{\phi})^{l}\epsilon^{(0)-}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{k}_{\phantom{-}l}(\partial_{\phi})^{l}\epsilon^{(0)}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=\frac{8\pi am}{\Xi^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,,\end{split}

(5.94)

and thus the total angular momentum for the hypersurface stretching between
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is

J=J_{+}+J_{-}=16\pi\frac{am}{(\Xi\kappa)^{2}}=2\frac{am}{\Xi^{2}G}\,.

(5.95)

By an identical argument for the mass charge we have

M=M_{-}+M_{+}=2M_{+}=16\pi\frac{m}{(\kappa\Xi)^{2}}=2\frac{m}{\Xi^{2}G}\,,

(5.96)

where we note that by an analogous argument to the Kruskalisation of SdS, we need to use the same vector field (
5.88
) in order to compute the contribution to the mass at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, noting that the directions of the vector field at
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
will be analogous to those displayed in Figure
10
.

6
Robinson-Trautman-dS
4

6.1
Overview

The final exact solution we will examine is that of the Robinson-Trautman de Sitter (RTdS) solution. The original solution
141
satisfies Einstein’s equations with no cosmological constant. The solution represents gravitational radiation from isolated compact objects and has many remarkable properties, see
154
;
43
;
89
for textbook discussions.

This solution is also of interest in the presence of a cosmological constant of either sign, see
53
for
\Lambda=0
,
29
for
\Lambda>0
and
30
for
\Lambda<0
. In recent years, the case of
\Lambda<0
has become the topic of much interest due to its role in the AdS/CFT correspondence and non-equilibrium dynamics
18
;
17
;
58
;
150
;
57
;
7
, as well as describing non-trivial gravitational radiation in AdS,
20
20

20

The Robinson-Trautman equation acts as a “Bondi mass loss” equation and is insensitive to the sign of
\Lambda
. For more information, see Section 3.3 of
18
.
an important first step in understanding fully the issue of gravitational waves in
\Lambda\neq 0
spacetimes.

As we expect the RT solution to provide an interesting example of non-trivial gravitational radiation in AldS spacetimes, it is natural to apply the techniques of Section
4
to see if flux formulae associated with the passage of gravitational waves arise. Before doing this, we begin with a brief overview of the spacetime, starting with the line element for the RTdS metric

ds^{2}=-F(r,u,z,\bar{z})du^{2}-2dudr+2r^{2}e^{\Phi(z,\bar{z};u)}dzd\bar{z}\,.

(6.1)

The coordinate
u
is a null retarded time coordinate
u\in(u_{0},\infty)
where
u_{0}>-\infty
is an initial null hypersurface,
r
is an affine radial distance and
(z,\bar{z})
are Kähler coordinates for the distorted 2-dimensional spheres which arise as surfaces of constant
u
and
r
. The front factor
F
is given by

F=r\partial_{u}\Phi-\Delta\Phi-\frac{2m}{r}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}r^{2}\,,

(6.2)

and

\Delta=e^{-\Phi}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\,,

(6.3)

denotes the Laplacian of the distorted 2-spheres. In order for the metric to satisfy the field equations (
2.4
),
\Phi
is required to satisfy the
Robinson-Trautman equation

3m\partial_{u}\Phi+\Delta\Delta\Phi=0\,,

(6.4)

an equation which has a close association to the Calabi flow equation on
S^{2}

40
;
41
, as was noted in
160
. In order to see this, one may consider the 2-metric

ds_{2}^{2}=2e^{\Phi}dzd\bar{z}\,,

(6.5)

which we note corresponds to the
r=1,u=\text{constant}
surface in (
6.1
). The Calabi flow equation on
S^{2}
takes the form

\partial_{u}g_{z\bar{z}}=\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}R_{2}\,,

(6.6)

where
R_{2}
is the Ricci curvature scalar of the metric (
6.5
). One may evaluate this equation explicitly as

\partial_{u}\Phi+2\Delta\Delta\Phi=0\,.

(6.7)

This is the Robinson-Trautman equation with
m=1/6
, which may be achieved by a choice of units. The Calabi flow is a well-studied subject in mathematics, and one can use several results concerning the flow to derive properties of Robinson-Trautman metrics. We will soon see how such properties will allow us to construct interesting examples of non-trivial gravitational charges for this spacetime.

We note that the RTdS solution reduces to the SdS solution (
5.7
) when one chooses

\Phi=\Phi_{0}\equiv-2\log\left(1+\frac{z\bar{z}}{2}\right)\,,

(6.8)

and makes use of the map

z=\sqrt{2}\cot\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)e^{i\phi}\,,\quad\bar{z}=\sqrt{2}\cot\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)e^{-i\phi}\,,

(6.9)

between the Kähler and spherical coordinates. We also note that in considering this limit we want the SdS solution to possess two distinct horizons
(r_{h},r_{c})
. This means that we will again impose the inequality (
5.8
) for the RTdS solutions.

The relationship between the RTdS and SdS is deeper than just a particular choice of
\Phi
in RTdS reducing to SdS. It has been shown in
50
(see also
29
) that the RTdS solution approaches the SdS metric exponentially fast as
u\rightarrow\infty
(equivalently the Calabi flow reaches a fixed point of the unit round
S^{2}
). This extension will generically not be smooth, but can be made “arbitrarily smooth” by considering the extremal value of
\ell\rightarrow 3\sqrt{3}m
in the Nariai bound (
5.8
)
29
. The smoothness of this extension will not be important for our purposes as we shall always work in the RTdS spacetime and will not consider the explicit extension beyond
u=\infty
. The fact that the solution settles down to SdS at late times will however be of importance.

These solutions are also an interesting class as they are explicitly AldS by virtue of the conformal class induced at the conformal boundary not being conformally flat
18
. In what follows we will examine these spacetimes at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Our formulae at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
will not be applicable for this spacetime as it does not possess a
\mathscr{I}^{-}
. The fact that the metric does not have a
\mathscr{I}^{-}
(and in fact has a naked singularity at
r=0
) is due to the fact that the metric is only supposed to accurately describe the gravitational field of a radiating body
outside
the source and does not include the (possibly very complicated) interior description, which should be included past
u=u_{0}
. This information is depicted via the Penrose diagram for the RTdS solution in Figure
12
. As we shall shortly see more explicitly, these spacetimes generically do not possess conformal Killing vectors at
\mathscr{I}^{+}

28
and thus we will use the modification procedure discussed in Section
3.2
in order to construct modified Hamiltonians.

Figure 12:
The Penrose diagram for the Robinson-Trautman de Sitter spacetime is highlighted in yellow. The spacetime starts at some initial retarded time
u=u_{0}
and evolves to the SdS solution at
u=\infty
. We have included the completion of the black hole interior of SdS solution past the
u=\infty
hypersurface, although this is not strictly part of the RTdS spacetime (one can extend through this surface, but not smoothly for generic
\Lambda

29
). We will consider the flux through a region of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, via calculation of the gravitational charges associated with the red timelike hypersurfaces.

6.2
Gravitational charges at future infinity

The aim is now to use this metric in order to compute the Hamiltonians (
4.119
) which occur at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In particular, we wish to evaluate

H_{\xi}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C}T^{k}_{l}\xi_{(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,,

(6.10)

a formula which will require us to obtain the Starobinsky expansion for RTdS spacetimes. Such a map has already been performed in partial detail in
29
(recovering
g_{(0)}
but not
g_{(3)}
as required for the Hamiltonians) and in full detail for RTAdS in
18
(see also
7
for a recent discussion). Here we will provide the full calculation for RTdS in order to obtain all necessary information required for computation of the Hamiltonians.

The map from Robinson-Trautman into Starobinsky coordinates takes the direct form given in
29

or can be done in stages following
18
. We will follow the second approach and start by transforming the line element (
6.1
) via the initial map

u=t-r_{*}\,,

(6.11)

where the tortoise coordinate
r_{*}
is that of the SdS solution (
5.38
). The full map then takes the form of

\displaystyle\begin{split}r_{*}&\rightarrow-\rho-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})\rho^{2}-\left[\frac{\Lambda}{9}+\frac{3}{16}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}+\frac{\Lambda}{12}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right]\rho^{3}-\frac{1}{432}\Big[8m\Lambda^{2}\\
&\qquad+27(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{3}+72\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi}(\Lambda+\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi})+36\partial_{t}^{3}\hat{\Phi}+12\Lambda(2\partial_{t}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})+3\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})\Big]\rho^{4}\\
&\qquad+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{5})\,,\\
t&\rightarrow t-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})\rho^{2}-\frac{1}{6}\left[\frac{2\Lambda}{3}+(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}+\partial^{2}_{t}\hat{\Phi}+\frac{2\Lambda}{3}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right]\rho^{3}-\frac{1}{32}\bigg[2(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{3}\\
&\qquad+\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi}\left(\frac{16\Lambda}{3}+5\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}\right)+2\partial_{t}^{3}\hat{\Phi}+\frac{2\Lambda}{3}(4\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi}+3\partial_{t}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}))\bigg]\rho^{4}+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{5})\,,\\
z&\rightarrow z+e^{-\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left[\frac{\Lambda}{18}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})\rho^{3}+\frac{\Lambda}{48}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\rho^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{5})\,,\\
\bar{z}&\rightarrow\bar{z}+e^{-\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left[\frac{\Lambda}{18}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})\rho^{3}+\frac{\Lambda}{48}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}+\frac{\Lambda}{3}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\rho^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{5})\,,\end{split}

(6.12)

where we note that our equation above is the dS analogue of equations (4.5)-(4.8) of
18
, including the reinstating of all factors of
\Lambda
in the
\mathcal{O}(\rho^{4})
term in the
r_{*}
map. We also use the notation of
18
in denoting
\hat{\Phi}
as the boundary value of
\Phi
,
i.e.

\hat{\Phi}(t,z,\bar{z})=\lim_{r_{*}\rightarrow 0}\Phi(u,z,\bar{z})\,,

(6.13)

where
\hat{\Phi}
satisfies the boundary RT equation

3m\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi}+\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}=0\,,

(6.14)

and
\hat{\Delta}
is the Laplacian of the constant-
t
cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{+}

\hat{\Delta}=e^{-\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\,.

(6.15)

After performing this change of variables, we have the following terms in the Starobinsky expansion (
2.11
) for the Robinson-Trautman spacetime

\displaystyle ds_{(0)}^{2}

\displaystyle=dt^{2}+\frac{6}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}dzd\bar{z}\,,

(6.16a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}ds_{(2)}^{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\Lambda}{3}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}-\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}\right]dt^{2}-\left[\frac{3}{4\Lambda}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}+\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right]e^{\hat{\Phi}}dzd\bar{z}\\
&\qquad-[(\partial_{t}\partial_{z}\hat{\Phi})dtdz]_{\text{c.c.}}\,,\end{split}

(6.16b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}ds_{(3)}^{2}&=-\frac{4m\Lambda^{2}}{27}dt^{2}+\frac{4m\Lambda}{9}e^{\hat{\Phi}}dzd\bar{z}\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{3}\left[\frac{2\Lambda}{3}\partial_{z}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})dtdz+\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{z}\hat{\Phi})^{2}-\partial_{z}^{2}\hat{\Phi}\right)dz^{2}\right]_{\text{c.c.}}\,,\end{split}

(6.16c)

where the subscript c.c. indicates that one also has to add the complex conjugate of the given term. This is all of the relevant information for the RTdS spacetime required for the computation of the charges and fluxes at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

Inspection of the
g_{(0)}
term (
6.16a
) shows that the RTdS spacetime is merely asymptotically locally dS and not asymptotically dS
18
. This can be shown via computation of the Cotton-York tensor

C^{ij}=\epsilon_{(0)}^{ikl}\nabla^{(0)}_{k}\left(R^{\phantom{(0)}j}_{(0)l}-\frac{1}{4}\delta^{j}_{l}R_{(0)}\right)\,,

(6.17)

where
\epsilon^{(0)}_{ijk}
is the volume element of
g_{(0)}
, oriented with positive direction in spherical coordinates
(\theta,\phi)

i.e.

\epsilon^{(0)}_{t\theta\phi}=\sqrt{g_{(0)}}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\sin\theta\csc^{4}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\quad\iff\quad\epsilon^{(0)}_{tz\bar{z}}=-i\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\,.

(6.18)

We can compute the components of the Cotton-York tensor to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{zz}=\bar{C}_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}=-\frac{i}{4}\partial_{t}\left((\partial_{z}\hat{\Phi})^{2}-2\partial_{z}^{2}\hat{\Phi}\right)\,,\quad C_{tz}=\bar{C}_{t\bar{z}}=-\frac{i}{6}\Lambda\partial_{z}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})\,,\end{split}

(6.19)

with all others vanishing. The fact that the Cotton-York tensor is non-zero confirms that this spacetime is not asymptotically dS and merely asymptotically locally dS as the conformal class at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is not conformally flat. We will also observe that (
6.16a
) generically admits no conformal Killing vectors and thus if we want to define notions of charges at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
then we will be forced to use the modified Hamiltonians (
3.19
).

We can perform the sanity check on our formula for the second non-vanishing term in the Starobinsky expansion (
6.16b
) which is given explicitly in terms of the leading order term
g_{(0)}
. We recall from (
2.22
) that

g_{(2)ij}=R_{(0)ij}-\frac{1}{4}R_{(0)}g_{(0)ij}\,,

(6.20)

and computing components we find

\displaystyle R_{(0)tt}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}\,,\quad\quad R_{(0)z\bar{z}}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\left(\frac{3}{\Lambda}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}+(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}\right)+2\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\,,

(6.21)

\displaystyle R_{(0)tz}

\displaystyle=\bar{R}_{t\bar{z}}^{(0)}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\partial_{z}\hat{\Phi}\,,\qquad R_{(0)}=-2\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{3}{2}(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})^{2}-\frac{2\Lambda}{3}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\,,

(6.22)

which reproduce (
6.16b
) via (
2.22
).

We can also derive the energy-momentum tensor at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
using (
4.41
), which as in the KdS case (
5.82
) becomes

T_{ij}=-\frac{3\ell^{2}}{2\kappa^{2}}g_{ij}^{(3)}\,,

(6.23)

due to the use of dimensionful time coordinate
\rho
. Using the values for the components of
g_{(3)}
as given in (
6.16c
) we find

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}T_{tt}

\displaystyle=\frac{2m\Lambda}{3}\,,\qquad\kappa^{2}T_{tz}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})\,,

(6.24)

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}T_{z\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=-me^{\hat{\Phi}}\,,\qquad\kappa^{2}T_{zz}=\frac{3}{4\Lambda}\partial_{t}\left((\partial_{z}\hat{\Phi})^{2}-2\partial_{z}^{2}\hat{\Phi}\right)\,,

(6.25)

with

T_{t\bar{z}}=\bar{T}_{tz}\,,\qquad T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}=\bar{T}_{zz}\,.

(6.26)

We can also verify that the energy-momentum tensor satisfies tracelessness (
4.38
)
T_{i}^{i}=0
and conservation (
4.39
)
\nabla^{(0)}_{i}T^{i}_{j}=0
, the latter of which is due to the boundary RT equation (
6.14
) in the following manner

\nabla_{i}^{(0)}T^{i}_{j}=\frac{1}{\ell^{2}\kappa^{2}}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}+3m\hat{\Phi}_{,t})\delta^{t}_{j}\,.

(6.27)

Finally, we note the relation between the components of the energy-momentum tensor and the Cotton tensor

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{zz}&=-i\frac{\Lambda}{3}\kappa^{2}T_{zz}\,,\qquad C_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}=i\frac{\Lambda}{3}\kappa^{2}T_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}\,,\\
C_{tz}&=-i\frac{\Lambda}{3}\kappa^{2}T_{tz}\,,\qquad C_{t\bar{z}}=i\frac{\Lambda}{3}\kappa^{2}T_{t\bar{z}}\,,\end{split}

(6.28)

and as in the AlAdS case
18
, all components are real when written in spherical coordinates
(\theta,\phi)
.

6.2.1
Mass-like gravitational charges

We can now compute the charge expression (
4.119
) for a vector field
\xi_{(0)}^{\mu}=\xi^{t}_{(0)}\delta^{\mu}_{t}
which is orthogonal to the constant-
t
cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We will see that the RTdS solution does not in general possess ACKVs and thus the existence criterion (
3.17
) will fail, and thus we may use the modification procedure discussed in Section
3.2
in order to define modified Hamiltonians, or (equivalently) use the formulae for charges derived in
Sections
4.4.1
-
4.4.2
, which satisfy flux-balance laws.

We start by recalling the metric induced at the conformal boundary (
6.16a
)

ds_{(0)}^{2}=dt^{2}+\frac{6}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}dzd\bar{z}\,,\qquad\epsilon^{(0)}_{tz\bar{z}}=-i\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}=-ie^{\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,,

(6.29)

where we performed the somewhat unusual rewriting of the volume form in order to separate the volume of the unit round
S^{2}
,
21
21

21

The factor of
i
that appears in (
6.29
) is related to the fact that
z,\bar{z}
are complex. The relationship with the usual spherical polar coordinates is (
6.9
).
where
\hat{\Phi}_{0}
is given by

e^{\hat{\Phi}_{0}}=\frac{1}{\left(1+\frac{z\bar{z}}{2}\right)^{2}}\,.

(6.30)

Using (
6.24
) together with (
6.29
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{\xi}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C}T^{k}_{l}\xi^{l}_{(0)}\epsilon^{(0)}_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\\
&=\int_{\partial C}T^{t}_{l}\xi^{l}_{(0)}\epsilon^{(0)}_{tz\bar{z}}\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}\xi_{(0)}^{t}e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}\,,\end{split}

(6.31)

where we write the unit round
S^{2}
volume element as
d\Omega_{0}=-ie^{\hat{\Phi}_{0}}dzd\bar{z}
. We also write the volume element on the generic distorted
S^{2}
as
d\Omega=-ie^{\hat{\Phi}}dzd\bar{z}
.

In order to evaluate (
6.31
), one needs to choose the vector field
\xi_{(0)}^{t}
. In order to gain intuition as to suitable choices of
\xi_{(0)}
, we study the form of the conformal Killing equations of the metric (
6.16a
). This will allow us to identify for which cases CKVs (and thus conserved charges) exist, as well as identifying suitable candidate vector fields to describe the passage of outgoing gravitational waves when no solutions exist. In order to simplify the presentation,
we write our vector field as

\xi_{(0)}^{i}\partial_{i}=f(t,z,\bar{z})\partial_{t}\,,

(6.32)

and attempt to solve the conformal Killing equations. We compute the components of the Lie derivative of the metric to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tt}

\displaystyle=2f_{,t}=2f_{,t}g^{(0)}_{tt}\,,

(6.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tz}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{zt}=f_{,z}\,,

(6.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{t\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}t}=f_{,\bar{z}}\,,

(6.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{z\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}z}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}\partial_{t}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}\right)f=f\hat{\Phi}_{,t}g^{(0)}_{z\bar{z}}\,,

(6.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{zz}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}=0\,,

(6.37)

which allows us to rewrite the conformal Killing equations as equations for
f
. We note that from the second and third lines above together with the fact that
g_{tz}=g_{t\bar{z}}=0
we have

f_{,z}=f_{,\bar{z}}=0\implies f=f(t)\,.

(6.38)

The remaining equation now comes from demanding that the factors multiplying the components of the metric in (
6.33
) and (
6.36
) are equivalent, giving us the equation

2f_{,t}=f\hat{\Phi}_{,t}\,,

(6.39)

which can be integrated

f(t)=c_{0}(z,\bar{z})\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\hat{\Phi}\right)\,,

(6.40)

where
c_{0}
is an integration “constant” that depends on angular variables,
z,\bar{z}
. Since
f
only depends on
t
this solution is only possible when

\hat{\Phi}(t,z,\bar{z})=\varphi_{0}(t)+\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(6.41)

for some
\varphi_{0}(t)
and
\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})
. Then the
z
and
\bar{z}
independence of
f
imply that

c_{0}(z,\bar{z})=c_{1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})}\,,

(6.42)

where
c_{1}
is a constant. Thus, RTdS spacetimes that admit asymptotic conformal Killing vectors (
6.32
) must have
\hat{\Phi}
of the form (
6.41
).
For such
\hat{\Phi}
the boundary Robinson-Trautman equation (
6.14
) implies,

3m\dot{\varphi}_{0}(t)+e^{-2\varphi_{0}(t)}e^{-\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(e^{-\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})\right)=0\,.

(6.43)

This PDE separates,

\displaystyle 3m\dot{\varphi}_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\lambda e^{-2\varphi_{0}(t)}\,;

(6.44)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(e^{-\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})\right)

\displaystyle=-\lambda e^{\varphi_{1}(z,\bar{z})}\,,

(6.45)

where
\lambda
is the separation constant. Integrating (
6.45
) over the sphere yields
\lambda=0
and thus from (
6.44
) and (
6.41
) we obtain

\hat{\Phi}_{,t}=0\,.

(6.46)

Then, either by solving (
6.45
) (with
\lambda=0
) or by using the fact that at late times the Robinson-Trautman-dS spacetime approaches the SdS solution
29
and thus
(due to the time-independence of
\hat{\Phi}
) the
\hat{\Phi}
must be that of SdS, we find

e^{\hat{\Phi}}=e^{\hat{\Phi}_{0}}=\frac{1}{\left(1+\frac{z\bar{z}}{2}\right)^{2}}\,.

(6.47)

We have shown that in order for an RTdS spacetime to admit asymptotic conformal Killing vectors of the form (
6.32
) it must be the SdS solution. We also note that in this case the conformal Killing vector,
\mathring{\xi}_{(0)}
, is strictly a Killing vector of the boundary and is given by
\mathring{\xi}^{i}_{(0)}\partial_{i}=\partial_{t}
. In the setting of a generic RTdS spacetime there are no conformal Killing vectors and
we will be using the results of Section

4.4
to define gravitational charges that satisfy flux-balance laws. Clearly there are (infinitely) many candidate vector fields,
\xi_{(0)}^{t}
which one may use to define a “mass”-like charge for the RTdS spacetime. In order to attach a physical meaning to the flux, it must be associated with a quantity which is conserved quantity. For example, if there is a solution of the conformal Killing equation in a neighborhood of the boundary, then one can defined a charge which is conserved there but its value changes outside this neighborhood, because flux carrying this charge enters or leaves that portion of the boundary. Taking this into account, we note that a sensible candidate must limit to the SdS time translation in the late time limit
i.e.

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\xi_{(0)}^{t}=1\,,

(6.48)

which is the only a priori assumption that we will place on our vector fields.

6.2.2
Angular-momentum-like gravitational charges

One can also construct “angular momentum” charges corresponding to vector fields
\varphi_{(0)}
, which are tangential to cross-sections of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Since these vectors are tangential to cross-sections, the existence property (
3.17
) is satisfied and thus we do not require use of the modification procedure. However, we note that these charges will not necessarily be radially invariant along the
t
-direction since they generically have
\left.\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)\right|_{B_{12}}\neq 0
. We write these Hamiltonians as

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{\varphi}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C}T^{k}_{l}\varphi^{l}_{(0)}\epsilon^{(0)}_{kij}\,dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\\
&=\int_{\partial C}T^{t}_{l}\varphi^{l}_{(0)}\epsilon^{(0)}_{tz\bar{z}}\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\varphi^{A}_{(0)}\partial_{A}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}\,,\end{split}

(6.49)

where captial Roman indices above denote a summation over the angular (
z,\bar{z}
) directions. We expect there to be no asymptotic conformal Killing vectors for the generic RTdS spacetime in the same manner as we found for the vector field
\xi
which was orthogonal to the cross-sections. We put an ansatz of the form

\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}=\varphi_{(0)}^{z}(t,z,\bar{z})\partial_{z}+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}(t,z,\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}\,,

(6.50)

with the late time condition of settling down to the axial Killing vector of the SdS solution

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}=\partial_{\phi}=i\left(z\partial_{z}-\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\right)\,,

(6.51)

i.e.

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{(0)}^{z}=iz
,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}=-i\bar{z}
. We evaluate the components of the Lie derivative of the metric as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tt}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tz}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{zt}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{t}\left(\varphi^{\bar{z}}_{(0)}\right)\,,

(6.53)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{t\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}t}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{t}\left(\varphi^{z}_{(0)}\right)\,,

(6.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{z\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}z}=\frac{3}{\Lambda}\partial_{A}\left(\varphi^{A}_{(0)}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\right)\,,

(6.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{zz}

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left(\varphi^{\bar{z}}_{(0)}\right)\,,

(6.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\Lambda}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\varphi^{z}_{(0)}\right)\,,

(6.57)

and we again attempt to solve the conformal Killing equation. The first equation above tells us that we need to strictly solve the Killing equation rather than the conformal Killing equation. In doing this, the second, third, fifth and sixth lines above reduce the ansatz of (
6.50
) to holomorphic and anti-holomorphic transformations on
S^{2}
,

\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}=\varphi_{(0)}^{z}(z)\partial_{z}+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}(\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}\,.

(6.58)

This only leaves the vanishing of (
6.55
) to be solved. We note that when (
6.55
) is satisfied, the Hamiltonian (
6.49
) appears to vanish. Indeed, upon integration by parts

H_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\varphi^{A}_{(0)}\partial_{A}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}=\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\partial_{A}\left(\varphi^{A}_{(0)}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\right)\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\,dzd\bar{z}=0\,.

(6.59)

However, one has to be careful with the total derivatives. Writing
\varphi_{(0)n}^{z}(z)=z^{n+1}
, the holomorphic vector fields
l_{n}=\varphi_{(0)n}^{z}(z)\partial_{z}
are globally defined on
S^{2}
only when
n=-1,0,1
(as is well-known, see for example
87
) - all other cases are singular either
z=0
or at
z\to\infty
. Unless
\hat{\Phi}
is such that the factor that multiplies
\varphi_{(0)n}^{z}
goes to zero sufficiently fast at the positions of the singularities, the integral in (
6.59
) is ill-defined. For generic
\hat{\Phi}
only the transformations with
n=-1,0,1
are well-defined. Similar comments apply to the anti-holomorphic transformations.

An example of RTdS spacetimes with a Killing vector of this form is that of axisymmetric solutions
i.e.
those for which
\partial_{\phi}\hat{\Phi}=0
and thus admit a Killing vector of the form
\mathring{\varphi}^{A}_{(0)}=\partial_{\phi}
. One can evaluate the Hamiltonian (
6.49
) as

H_{\mathring{\varphi}}=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\partial_{\phi}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}=0\,,

(6.60)

demonstrating that axisymmetric Robinson-Trautman metrics have zero angular momentum.

For generic
\hat{\Phi}
which satisfies (
6.14
) there will be no Killing vectors and one will again be forced to deal with quantities which admit some flux across
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. As in the case of the “mass” (
6.31
), the angular momentum charge in RTdS spacetime thus has an infinite family of candidates based on vector fields which satisfy the late time condition (
6.51
).

6.3
Fluxes

As we have discussed, there are generically no asymptotic conformal Killing vectors associated with RTdS spacetimes. This means that the gravitational charges (
6.31
), (
6.49
) will not remain constant as one varies the coordinate
t
along
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, but rather they would satisfy the flux-balance law

\left.H_{\xi}\right|_{C_{2}}-\left.H_{\xi}\right|_{C_{1}}=-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\xi_{(0)}}\,,

(6.61)

where
B_{12}
is the strip of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
between
C_{1}
, see Figure
5
, and
C_{2}
and the flux is given by

\bm{F}_{\xi}=-\frac{1}{2}T^{ij}\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{ij}\,\bm{\epsilon}^{(0)}\,,

(6.62)

and
\xi_{(0)}
is the boundary value of the bulk vector
\xi
, as in (
4.70
).
We will now analyse the flux formulae for the mass type vector field (
6.32
) and the angular momentum type vector field (
6.50
) individually.
The flux for the Hamiltonian for a general linear combination of these fields can then be deduced via the suitable linear combination of the fluxes.

There are two important cases that we discuss below:

•

The integral of the flux vanishes
. This implies that the gravitational charge is
conserved
. We discussed earlier in Section
4.4
that a sufficient condition is that the boundary metric admits CKVs. In this case the flux (
6.62
) vanishes point-wise.
However, a sufficient condition for conservation is that the integral vanishes and this only requires that
{\bf F}_{\xi}
is exact over the compact directions.

•

The integral of the flux is sign-definite
. In this case the charges are not conserved, but they are
monotonic
. An important example is the Bondi mass-loss formula, and we will discuss generalisations.

6.3.1
Mass fluxes

We first compute the flux formula for the generic vector field (
6.32
). Using equations (
6.33
)-(
6.36
) together with the expressions for the energy-momentum tensor (
6.24
)-(
6.26
) and (
6.18
) for the volume form we can write the flux form as

\bm{F}_{\xi}=\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\left[\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)f_{,\bar{z}}+\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)f_{,z}+4me^{\hat{\Phi}}f_{,t}-2me^{\hat{\Phi}}f\hat{\Phi}_{,t}\right]dt\wedge dz\wedge d\bar{z}\,,

(6.63)

and the flux-balance equation (
6.62
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{t_{1}}-\mathcal{H}_{\xi}|_{t_{0}}&=-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{F}_{\xi}\\
&=\frac{i}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\left[(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})f-2mf_{,t}+mf\hat{\Phi}_{,t}\right]\,dtdzd\bar{z}\,,\end{split}

(6.64)

where we integrated by parts in the angular coordinates and ignored total derivatives to simplify the first term on the second line. This term is now in the ideal form to apply the boundary RT equation (
6.14
). Application of this, followed by some further manipulations allows us to bring the flux formula into the form

-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{F}_{\xi}=-\frac{2mi}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{t}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}f\right)\,dtdzd\bar{z}\,,

(6.65)

which is in agreement with the obvious formula for flux between two cuts which one could obtain via Stokes’ theorem.

6.3.2
Angular momentum fluxes

We now compute the flux form (
6.62
) for the vector field given by (
6.50
). The general expression for the flux form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\varphi}&=-\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}\Lambda}\left\{2m\Lambda\left[\partial_{z}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}\varphi_{(0)}^{z}\right)+\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}\right)\right]-\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.3\left[(\varphi_{(0)}^{z})_{,\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Phi}_{,z}\hat{\Phi}_{,tz}-\hat{\Phi}_{,tzz}\right)+(\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}})_{,z}\left(\hat{\Phi}_{,\bar{z}}\hat{\Phi}_{,t\bar{z}}-\hat{\Phi}_{,t\bar{z}\bar{z}}\right)\right.\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left.\left.+(\varphi_{(0)}^{z})_{,t}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)+(\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}})_{,t}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right]\right\}\,dt\wedge dz\wedge d\bar{z}\,,\end{split}

(6.66)

which we can simplify further by integrating over a strip of the conformal boundary

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\varphi}&=-\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{B_{12}}\left[(\varphi_{(0)}^{z})_{,t}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)+\varphi_{(0)}^{z}\partial_{t}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right)\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}})_{,t}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}\partial_{t}\left(e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right)\right]\,dtdzd\bar{z}\\
&=-\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{t}\left(\varphi_{(0)}^{z}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right)\,dtdzd\bar{z}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{t}\left(\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\right)\,dtd\Omega_{0}\,,\end{split}

(6.67)

giving the expected result when comparing with the Hamiltonians (
6.49
). In deriving the above equation, we used the fact that total derivatives in
z,\bar{z}
integrate to zero. This fact meant that the first line of (
6.66
) vanishes when integrated over the angular directions. We then use integration by parts in
z
and
\bar{z}
on the second line of (
6.66
) and after some rearrangement arrive at the above. Note that we did not require the Robinson-Trautman equation (
6.14
) in order to arrive at the expected result.

6.4
Examples of conserved and monotonic gravitational charges

We now give several interesting examples of conserved and monotonic gravitational charges for the RTdS solution. We shall present several vector fields associated with conserved charges, despite not admitting any non-trivial conformal Killing vectors. We shall see that these charges are invariant not by virtue of the flux form (
6.63
) vanishing, but rather it being a total derivative over the cross-sections
i.e.

\mathbf{F}=d_{2}\mathbf{a}\wedge dt\,,

(6.68)

where
d_{2}
is the exterior derivative associated with the metric (
6.73
) and
\mathbf{a}
a one-form. Our analysis suggests that in order to look for conserved charges of general spacetimes, one should search for solutions with flux of the form (
6.68
) rather than the more restrictive condition of
\mathbf{F}=0
. We will also give examples of charges that are monotonically decreasing in boundary coordinate
t
. We provide a further example of a non-monotonic charge in Appendix
G
, where we compare our formalism with the linearised results of
47
;
113
;
46
;
34
.

6.4.1
Conserved charges

Cross-section area

The first choice we make is

\xi^{t}_{(0)}=f=1\,,

(6.69)

which satisfies (
6.48
) and gives the gravitational charge (
6.31
)

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}\,d\Omega=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}A_{2}\,,

(6.70)

where we note that
A_{2}
is the area of the cross-section
\partial C
. Evaluation of the flux form (
6.63
) gives

\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\xi}=-\frac{i}{\kappa^{2}}m\int_{B_{12}}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\hat{\Phi}_{,t}\,dtdzd\bar{z}=\frac{i}{3\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\,dtdzd\bar{z}=0\,,

(6.71)

where we used the boundary RT equation (
6.14
) in going from the second to the third expression and then the fact that the resulting integrand is a total derivative over a closed surface in order to reach the conclusion of zero flux. We note that the conservation of the area follows directly from the Robinson-Trautman equation, and is in fact a general property of two-metrics evolving under Calabi flow
40
;
41
.

Euler characteristic

The second choice we make is

\xi^{t}_{(0)}=f=-\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\,,

(6.72)

which satisfies (
6.48
) by virtue of
\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}_{0}=-1
. We note that this choice is motivated by the scalar curvature of the metric

ds_{2}^{2}=2e^{\hat{\Phi}(z,\bar{z};t)}dzd\bar{z}\,,

(6.73)

which is found to be

R_{2}=-2\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\,.

(6.74)

The gravitational charge (
6.31
) is

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=\frac{m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}R_{2}\,d\Omega=\frac{m}{G}\chi=2\frac{m}{G}\,,

(6.75)

which is proportional to the Euler characteristic
\chi=2
by the Gauss-Bonnet theorem. Clearly this quantity is constant, a fact which can be confirmed using the integral of the flux form (
6.63
) for a vector field given by (
6.72
). Several applications of the RT equation yield

\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\xi}=-2m\frac{i}{\kappa^{2}}\int\partial_{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi}\,dtdzd\bar{z}=0\,,

(6.76)

again via Stokes’ theorem for integration over the closed
(z,\bar{z})
-surface.

Angular momentum and Lorentz charges

We now turn to study some interesting examples of the angular momentum like Hamiltonians (
6.49
). A natural first choice of vector field to analyse is

\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}=\partial_{\phi}=i\left(z\partial_{z}-\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\right)\,,

(6.77)

where we recall that this is the unique vector field (
6.58
) which solves all components of the Killing equation other than the
z\bar{z}
component (given by (
6.55
)) and satisfies the asymptotic property (
6.51
). The Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{\varphi}&=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\left[z\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)-\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right]e^{\hat{\Phi}}\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=\frac{1}{4\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\left[-\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(z(\hat{\Phi}_{,z})^{2}\right)+\partial_{z}\left(\bar{z}(\hat{\Phi}_{,\bar{z}})^{2}\right)\right]\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=0\,,\end{split}

(6.78)

which is obviously radially invariant. As a consistency check we analyse the flux form

\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\varphi}=-\frac{im}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}\left[\partial_{z}\left(ie^{\hat{\Phi}}z\right)-\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(ie^{\hat{\Phi}}\bar{z}\right)\right]\,dtdzd\bar{z}=0\,,

(6.79)

where we used Stokes’ theorem in order to ignore the total derivatives. This example illustrates that even when
\partial_{\phi}
is not a symmetry of the Robinson-Trautman spacetime, it gives a vanishing Hamiltonian charge.

In fact, even if we were to relax the asymptotic property (
6.51
) then the corresponding Hamiltonian still vanishes. Explicitly, we take

\varphi_{(0)}^{A}\partial_{A}=\varphi_{(0)}^{z}(z)\partial_{z}+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}(\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}\,,

(6.80)

which is the sum of holomorphic,
\varphi_{(0)}^{z}(z)\partial_{z}
and anti-holomorphic,
\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}(\bar{z})\partial_{\bar{z}}
vector fields that are globally well-defined:

\varphi_{(0)}^{z}(z)=a_{0}+a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}\,,\quad\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}(\bar{z})=b_{0}+b_{1}\bar{z}+b_{2}\bar{z}^{2}\,,

(6.81)

where the coefficients
a_{0,1,2},b_{0,1,2}
are constants. This six dimensional space of vectors forms a basis for the Lorentz algebra and thus
we will call globally defined vectors of the form (
6.80
)
Lorentz vector fields
. The Hamiltonian corresponding to any of these vectors reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{\varphi}&=-\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\left[\varphi_{(0)}^{z}\partial_{z}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)+\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi}\right)\right]e^{\hat{\Phi}}\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=\frac{i}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{3}{\Lambda}\int_{\partial C}\left[\partial_{\bar{z}}\left((\varphi_{(0)}^{z})_{,z}\hat{\Phi}_{,z}\right)+\partial_{z}\left((\varphi_{(0)}^{\bar{z}}),_{\bar{z}}\hat{\Phi}_{,\bar{z}}\right)\right]\,dzd\bar{z}\\
&=0\,,\end{split}

(6.82)

and we thus conclude that the Lorentz charges of the RTdS metric vanish. We can again verify this via examination of the integrated flux form

\int_{B_{12}}\mathbf{F}_{\varphi}=-\frac{im}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{A}\left(\varphi_{(0)}^{A}e^{\hat{\Phi}}\right)\,dtdzd\bar{z}=0\,.

(6.83)

We note that even though (
6.80
) is not a Killing vector of (
6.16a
), it does satisfy all of the Killing equations other than (
6.55
) which is a total derivative. The fact that
T^{z\bar{z}}{\epsilon}_{(0)}
(present in equation (
6.62
)) is constant then immediately forces the flux form to be exact and thus the flux vanishes. One could construct additional charges with non-zero value and flux properties. We leave the investigation of such charges and their physical properties for future work.

Another interesting future direction is to relax the condition that the vector field (
6.80
) is globally defined and allow for local vector fields with meromorphic singularities. This would parallel the discussion of extended BMS vector fields for asymptotically flat spacetimes considered in
24
;
22
;
23
(in turn inspired by
27
). Such charges would certainly be relevant
for the cases where the solution of the RT equation is such that the integrals over
S^{2}
that define the charges are finite despite the singularity in the vector field, and they may also have a role to play more generally.

6.4.2
Monotonic charges

We now give several examples of monotonic charges w.r.t. evolution in
t
:

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{H}_{\xi}\leq 0\,.

(6.84)

Bondi mass

The first of our monotonically decreasing charge is one which we will refer to as the
Bondi mass

33
. The Bondi mass was originally defined in
asymptotically flat
spacetime, although it has been shown
18
that an analogous quantity can be defined in RTAdS spacetime, exhibiting physically sensible properties such as being a monotonically decreasing function of the boundary time. Here we will show that such a quantity can be constructed as gravitational charge for RTdS following the analysis in Section
4.4
, and that it satisfies the monotonicity property (
6.84
).

The choice of vector field is

\xi^{t}_{(0)}=f=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}(z,\bar{z};t)}\,,

(6.85)

where (
6.48
) is clearly satisfied and where our use of the function
\hat{\sigma}
matches
18
, where it was introduced to measure the discrepancy between the general RT metric and the SdS solution (
\hat{\sigma}=1
is SdS). One can check that the variation induced by this vector field rescales the
tt
and
z\bar{z}
components of the metric by the same factor
i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tt}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})g_{tt}^{(0)}\,,

(6.86)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{z\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)(\partial_{t}\hat{\Phi})g^{(0)}_{z\bar{z}}\,,

(6.87)

but it fails to be a conformal Killing vector by virtue of

\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{tz}=\partial_{z}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)=\partial_{z}\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}}\right)\neq 0\,,

(6.88)

and

\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{(0)}}g^{(0)}_{t\bar{z}}=\partial_{\bar{z}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)=\partial_{\bar{z}}\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}}\right)\neq 0\,.

(6.89)

Inserting the vector field (
6.85
) into (
6.31
) we find the modified Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{C\cap\mathscr{I}^{+}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(3\hat{\Phi}-3\hat{\Phi}_{0})\right)\,d\Omega_{0}=\frac{m}{4\pi G}\int_{C\cap\mathscr{I}^{+}}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{3}}\,d\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{Bondi}}\,,

(6.90)

which is precisely the Bondi mass integral as given in equation (3.30) of
18
.

The flux formula in this case gives

-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{F}_{\xi}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{B_{12}}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{3}}\right)\,dtd\Omega_{0}\leq 0\,,

(6.91)

where the final inequality comes from
149
;
50
;
51
(see Appendix A of
50
for a proof of this result). The aformentioned work was for the asymptotically flat RT spacetime and the inequality was used in order to prove that the Bondi mass is monotonically decreasing in the RT spacetime, but the monotonicity proof only uses the RT equation (
6.4
) which is independent of
\Lambda
. The proof thus extends immediately to the
\Lambda\neq 0
cases and thus the flux formula gives a monotonically decreasing quantity.

Calabi functional

A second interesting example of a monotonic charge arises from the choice of

\xi_{(0)}^{t}=f=(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})^{2}\,,

(6.92)

which again satisfies the asymptotic condition (
6.48
) and gives the modified Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})^{2}\,d\Omega=\frac{m}{2\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}(R_{2})^{2}\,d\Omega=\frac{m}{2\kappa^{2}}\mathcal{C}\,,

(6.93)

where we read off the Calabi functional
\mathcal{C}
on the right hand side. Examination of the flux form gives

-\int_{B_{12}}\bm{F}_{\xi}=\frac{m}{2\kappa^{2}}\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{C}\leq 0\,,

(6.94)

where we used the fact that the Calabi functional is monotonically decreasing along the flow. For a nice proof of this, see
e.g.
Appendix A of
50
. We note again that this monotonicity only depends on the Calabi flow (RT equation) which is independent of
\Lambda
.

Other monotonically decreasing quantities

In the two preceding examples we have been able to recover well-known monotonically decreasing quantities associated with the RT spacetime as modified Hamiltonians at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. One may be interested in constructing other quantities that obey monotonicity, with a further example being the choice

\xi_{(0)}^{t}=f=\hat{\Phi}\,,

(6.95)

which we note does not satisfy (
6.48
) and as such it is not a good candidate to describe the loss of energy through
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In any case, this vector field leads to a modified Hamiltonian of the form

\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}\hat{\Phi}\,d\Omega\,,

(6.96)

which admits a monotonically decreasing flux via (
6.63
)

-\int\mathbf{F}_{\xi}=-\frac{2}{3\kappa^{2}}\int(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})^{2}\,dtd\Omega\leq 0\,.

(6.97)

While this monotonic charge is not related to the loss of energy of the system (due to the aformentioned failing of (
6.48
)), this construction highlights how one may use the flux-balance law in order to construct potentially novel monotonically decreasing functionals associated with the spacetime.

We also note that the construction of gravitational charges as discussed in this work may allow one to understand global aspects of the RTdS metrics using their properties. Interesting global issues for RTdS metrics include the presence and properties of a past apparent horizon (see
160
for
\Lambda=0
and
18
for
\Lambda<0
cases) and whether the Bondi mass of the spacetime satisfies a Penrose inequality
130
(see
159
for
\Lambda=0
and
18
for
\Lambda<0
) or Thorne’s hoop conjecture
158
(see
86
;
65
for
\Lambda=0
,
18
for
\Lambda<0
). The study of these topics for RTdS spacetimes is at a less-advanced stage relative to their
\Lambda\leq 0
cousins, although this work shows that several of the equations describing these global issues may now be expressed in terms of gravitational charges (such as (
6.90
)) associated with RTdS spacetimes. It would be interesting to investigate whether the properties of the charges discussed in this work would help to make headway in understanding global aspects of RTdS metrics.

7
Conclusions and outlook

We have presented a comprehensive analysis of gravitational charges in asymptotically locally de Sitter spacetimes. There are similarities and differences relative to asymptotically locally AdS spacetimes. The local analysis near the conformal boundary is linked to that of AdS by analytic continuation. In the AldS case, however, the spatial sections are compact and conformal infinity is now at past and future infinity, and the gravitational charges are defined using two-ended timelike hypersurfaces. In general, the overall Hamiltonian receives
non-trivial contributions from both
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, and while classically causality and the presence of a cosmological horizon implies that
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and
\mathscr{I}^{-}
may be treated separately, the global nature of the Hamiltonian may become important when analysing the symmetry constraints on the transition amplitude from
\mathscr{I}^{-}
and
\mathscr{I}^{+}
at the quantum level.

We provided a first principle derivation of the gravitational charges and the flux-balance law they satisfy, and we illustrated our analysis using several examples. A necessary and sufficient condition for conservation of the charge is that the gravitational flux vanishes. This includes the case where the conformal boundary admits conformal Killing vectors, but (surprisingly) we also find examples where the conservation is not linked with an asymptotic conformal Killing vector. In such cases, the flux density is not vanishing but it is exact over compact directions so that its integral vanishes.

Examples of such conserved charges appeared in our analysis of the RTdS spacetime at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The RTdS possesses a non-trivial conformal structure at infinity, which (in general) does not admit conformal Killing vectors. Nevertheless, by studying the vanishing of the flux we were able to establish the existence of two conserved quantities. To understand their meaning, recall that the RTdS solution is obtained by solving the RT equation, which is the same equation that governs the Calabi flaw on
S^{2}
. It is a mathematical fact that the Calabi flow is area preserving and it does not change the topology of space,
i.e.
the genus of the surface is invariant under the flow. The two conserved charges are precisely the area of the deformed
S^{2}
and its genus. It would be interesting to find other solutions that exhibit analogous conserved charges.

The flux-balance formulae allow us also to systematically investigate the existence of monotonic charges, i.e. charges that change monotonically: the existence of such charges is related to flux of definite sign. In the context of radiation, a particularly important example of such a charge is the Bondi mass. We have indeed identified a monotonically decreasing charge that can be identified with the Bondi mass (and it is equal to the standard Bondi mass for the
\Lambda=0
RT solution). It is well-known that the Calabi flow possesses a functional that is decreasing along the flow. We have identified the corresponding gravitational charge, and also identified yet another monotonic charge, which appears to be new. It would be interesting to understand the implications of the existence of this charge for the Calabi flow.

We found several interesting functionals as gravitational charges for the RTdS spacetime, but a full systematic classification of the asymptotic symmetry structure is still lacking. The asymptotic conditions of (
6.48
) and (
6.51
) that the RTdS spacetime reduces to SdS as
u\rightarrow\infty
are useful but not restrictive enough to identify unique quantities which describe the arrival of gravitational radiation at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In choosing our vector fields in Section
6.4
we often made use of the fact that several, but not all, of the components of the conformal Killing equation were satisfied. A potentially important observation is that the vector field (
6.85
) which gives the Bondi mass Hamiltonian (
6.90
) is an example of an
off-shell
conformal Killing vector of (
6.16a
) in that it solves the conformal Killing equation as long as
\hat{\Phi}
does not satisfy the boundary RT equation. The physical properties of the gravitational charges associated with such a vector field suggest that the strategy of constructing off-shell conformal Killing vectors and analysing the corresponding charges may be a fruitful avenue in understanding the properties of gravitational radiation in AldS spacetimes more generally.

In this paper we focused our analysis on pure gravity in four dimensions. Natural extensions include the addition of matter, including Maxwell, scalar fields,
etc.
. Extending to higher dimensions would also be interesting. In odd dimensions, we would need to deal with the analogue of the holographic conformal anomaly
98
;
127
.
In our analysis, we presented three different derivations of the conserved charges: two based on the first and second Noether theorem and the third on covariant phase space. We have shown that the expressions for the charges were equivalent, but for this to be the case we needed to include a corner term in the definition of the charge localized in the intersection of
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
and the hypersurface
C
. This allowed us to relax a technical assumption made in
127
. It would be interesting to work out the corner contribution in other dimensions as well.
