We discussed quantities that satisfy flux-balance laws with respect to temporal translations. It would be important to further improve the discussion of spacetime fluxes when the past end lies in the spacetime interior as discussed in Section
4.5.2
. In particular, it would be useful to understand better the boundary condition at interior surfaces that are induced dynamically by the time evolution. Related to this, it would be good to understand the physics behind the fixing of the ambiguity
W
as presented in (
4.174
), for example, by making reasonable physical assumptions on the nature of sources and outgoing radiation,
etc.
. This would allow one to construct a description of bulk gravitational radiation in AldS spacetimes.

One way of excluding incoming radiation is suggested in
13
(the surface should be a non-expanding horizon) and this condition may be expressible in terms of the ambiguity
W
that one adds to the past end. In order to explore this fully, one would need to extend the discussion of spacelike separated past ends contained in this work to include lightlike separated past ends. This would allow us to consider null surfaces such as the cosmological horizon as the past end
13
. The works
47
;
46
;
49
consider Hamiltonians associated with null surfaces in AldS spacetimes and may be of use in this endeavor.

There have been several recent works that generalize concepts from
electromagnetic radiation, such as the Poynting vector,
etc.
, to the gravitational setting
77
;
76
;
170
;
78
;
81
;
75
;
57
;
7
;
80
. It would be interesting to understand the relation to our flux-balance formulae. Their approach appears to relate radiation to linear independence of energy-momentum and Cotton tensors
i.e.
for
\Lambda>0
the condition given is that “there is no gravitational radiation on the open portion
B_{12}\subset\mathscr{I}^{+}
if and only if
C_{ij}
and
T_{ij}
commute”. For the RTdS spacetime (
6.28
) can be used to show that the tensors do not commute and thus the RTdS spacetime has gravitational radiation, consistent with our observations as well as the recent work
7
. It would be interesting to explore more deeply the relation between this condition and our analysis, in particular to study whether the condition can be utilised to understand the space of conformal Killing vectors of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
for a spacetime. This may then allow for a direct connection between the super-Poynting vector and the language of charges and fluxes as developed in this work.

The definition of gravitational charges makes use of the
T^{t}_{i}
components of the energy-momentum tensor. In non-relativistic physics the space-space components of
T_{ij}
encode the stresses that act on bodies: the force in the
i
-direction that acts on a surface whose normal is
j
with diagonal elements representing normal stress and off-diagonals shear. It would be interesting to extract in our case the physics associated with these components of
T_{ij}
. In the example of the RTdS spacetime, and from the perspective of a boundary fluid, such components correspond to the viscous part of the energy-momentum tensor
18
;
58
;
150
;
7
and describe higher order corrections to the ideal hydrodynamic behaviour.

Another future direction is to investigate whether one can use the technology introduced in this paper to study more advanced aspects of radiation, such as the gravitational memory effect in de Sitter. In asymptotically flat spacetime it has been shown that the memory effect can be understood as a vacuum transition between spacetimes related by asymptotic symmetries
84
;
155
;
129
. It would be interesting to see if one can characterise the memory effect in AldS spacetimes in a similar manner.
The study of the memory effect in de Sitter settings is a topic of some recent interest
108
;
161
;
82
;
92
;
35
;
73
;
139
and it would be interesting to examine how our formalism relates to these approaches.

Last but not least, it would be interesting to extract that implication of the charges at the quantum level.

Appendix A
Useful results on the covariant phase space

We provide proofs for a number of results concerning the covariant phase space that were stated in the main text.

A.1
Symplectic current is closed on-shell

We can rewrite equation (
3.5
) as

d\bm{\omega}\approx 0\,,

(A.1)

a result which we will prove following
117
;
164
(a nice recap is also given in
127
). We start by noting that two variations of the Lagrangian gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\mathbf{L}&=\delta_{1}(\mathbf{E}\delta_{2}\psi+d\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{2}\psi))\\
&=\delta_{1}\mathbf{E}\delta_{2}\psi+\mathbf{E}\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\psi+\delta_{1}d\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)\\
&\approx d\delta_{1}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)\,,\end{split}

(A.2)

where we used (
3.2
) as well as the commutativity of the variation
\delta_{1}
and the spacetime exterior derivative
d
. By an almost identical line of argument we can write

\displaystyle\delta_{2}\delta_{1}\mathbf{L}\approx d\delta_{2}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi)\,,

(A.3)

and now using the fact that the variation operators commute (
[\delta_{1},\delta_{2}]=0
) we are able to write

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\mathbf{L}-\delta_{2}\delta_{1}\mathbf{L}&\approx d\delta_{1}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)-d\delta_{2}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi)=d\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta_{1}\psi,\delta_{2}\psi)\,,\end{split}

(A.4)

which completes the proof.

A.2
Variation of Noether current

The variation of the Noether current form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta\mathbf{J}[\xi]&=\delta\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\delta(i_{\xi}\mathbf{L})\\
&=\delta\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\delta\mathbf{L}\\
&=\delta\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}[d\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)+\mathbf{E}(\psi)\delta\psi]\\
&=\delta\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)+di_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{E}(\psi)\delta\psi\\
&=\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)+di_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{E}(\psi)\delta\psi\,.\end{split}

(A.5)

In going from the first to the second line we have used the property of
\xi
being fixed and from the second to the third line we have used (
3.2
). In third to the fourth lines we have applied the identity
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}=i_{\xi}d+di_{\xi}
for the Lie derivative acting on differential forms and finally we have applied (
3.4
) in order to reinstate
\bm{\omega}
. One can now easily see that by going on-shell,
\mathbf{E}\approx 0
, equation (
3.10
) is immediately obtained.

More generally than the on-shell result (
3.9
), it has been shown in
105
that we can write

\mathbf{J}[\xi]=d\mathbf{Q}[\xi]+i_{\xi}\mathbf{C}(\psi)\,,

(A.6)

where
\mathbf{C}
is a
d
-form which vanishes on-shell (physically this form vanishing is fulfillment of the constraint equations of the theory
117
). We can use (
A.6
) to simplify (
A.5
) via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)&=\delta(d\mathbf{Q}[\xi]+i_{\xi}\mathbf{C}(\psi))-d(i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi))+i_{\xi}\mathbf{E}(\psi)\delta\psi\\
&=d[\delta\mathbf{Q}[\xi]-i_{\xi}\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\delta\psi)]+i_{\xi}\delta\mathbf{C}(\psi)+i_{\xi}\mathbf{E}(\psi)\delta\psi\,,\end{split}

(A.7)

and now going on-shell at the level of both the equations of motion and the linearised equations of motion, the final two terms vanish and one immediately arrives at equation (
3.11
).

Appendix B
Holographic counterterms from the Hamiltonian approach

We derive the explicit form of the counterterm action (
4.33
). In doing this, we demonstrate that this method is completely equivalent to the Lagrangian approach to holographic renormalisation for AldS spacetimes as initially discussed in
152
and more recently considered in the context of asymptotic charges in
59
;
114
.

In order to compute the counterterm action (
4.33
), we merely need to compute the eigenfunctions of the dilatation operator
K_{(0)},K_{(2)}
. The first one of these is easily computed using (
4.37
) and taking the trace of the first equation, resulting in

K_{(0)}=3\,.

(B.1)

The second term,
K_{(2)}
, requires a little more work as simply tracing over the second equation of (
4.37
) gives an expression which is not manifestly covariant in terms of the induced metric on the constant
\tau
hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau}
. In order to derive a covariant expression for
K_{(2)}
, we plug the expansions (
4.27
) into the first equation of (
4.19
) and solve order by order in dilatation weight. The equation at weight 2 reads
22
22

22

R[\gamma]=R_{(2)}[\gamma]
by construction.

2K_{(0)i}^{\phantom{(0)}j}K_{(2)j}^{\phantom{(2)}i}-2K_{(0)}K_{(2)}=R[\gamma]\,,

(B.2)

and using the first equation in (
4.37
), we arrive at the result

K_{(2)}=-\frac{R[\gamma]}{4}\,,

(B.3)

and thus the counterterm action (
4.33
) is

S_{\text{ct}}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}d^{3}x\sqrt{\gamma}\left(2-\frac{R[\gamma]}{2}\right)\,,

(B.4)

precisely in agreement with those of
20
;
152
;
59
;
114
.

Appendix C
Finiteness of the Wald Hamiltonian at
\mathscr{I}^{+}

We prove the formula (
4.119
) for the Hamiltonian defined at a cross-section of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, starting from the final line of (
4.118
). Clearly all we need to show is

I=\int_{\partial C}\left(v^{k}_{(2)}+\sum_{n=0}^{2}(K^{\phantom{(n)}k}_{(n)l}-\lambda_{(n)}\delta^{k}_{l})\xi^{l}\right)\epsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=0\,,

(C.1)

which we will now do. We note that this proof follows very closely to that of the AlAdS case considered in appendix C of
127
. Although now we will be able to extend the result to spacetimes without additional isometries due to the inclusion of the corner term induced by the variation of the holographic counterterms. We begin with the definition (
3.7
), which using (
3.9
) we can write on-shell as

d\mathbf{Q}[\xi]=\bm{\Theta}(\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\psi)-i_{\xi}\mathbf{L}_{\text{on-shell}}\,,

(C.2)

or equivalently using (
4.6
) and (
4.111
) as

-d*\mathbf{\Xi}=*\bm{v}-*\bm{\xi}L_{\text{on-shell}}\,,

(C.3)

where we introduced
\mathbf{L}_{\text{on-shell}}=L_{\text{on-shell}}\bm{\epsilon}
.
We can take an additional Hodge star of this equation to obtain

\displaystyle-*d*\mathbf{\Xi}=**\left(\bm{v}-\bm{\xi}L_{\text{on-shell}}\right)\,,

(C.4)

or equivalently

\nabla_{\nu}\Xi^{\nu\mu}=v^{\mu}-\xi^{\mu}L_{\text{on-shell}}\,.

(C.5)

We use the fact that the on-shell action can be written as

L_{\text{on-shell}}=\frac{3}{8\pi G}=\frac{1}{8\pi G}(\dot{\lambda}+K\lambda)\,,

(C.6)

and study the boundary component of (
C.5
)

\frac{1}{8\pi G}\partial_{\tau}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}(K^{i}_{j}-\delta^{i}_{j}\lambda)\xi^{j}\right)=\partial_{j}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\Xi^{ji}\right)-\sqrt{\gamma}v^{i}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2\tau})\,,

(C.7)

where we made use of (
4.17
), (
4.73
) and (
4.113
) in manipulating (
C.5
). The equation above will be the crucial result in proving (
C.1
).
In order to do this, we will expand each side of the equation in eigenfunctions of
\delta_{D}
, the dilatation operator, and equate terms of the same order. We have already seen that the time derivative is very closely related to this operator (
4.35
) and following
126
;
127
we can formally expand this operator as

\partial_{\tau}=\delta_{D}+(\text{terms which vanish on}\>\mathscr{I^{+}})\,.

(C.8)

Using our expansions (
4.27
) for
K^{i}_{j}
and
\lambda
as well as the dilatation weight expansions

\Xi^{ij}=\Xi^{ij}_{(2)}+\ldots\,,\qquad v^{i}=v^{i}_{(2)}+\ldots\,,

(C.9)

we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\gamma}(K_{(0)j}^{\phantom{(0)}i}-\delta^{i}_{j}\lambda_{(0)})\xi^{j}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(C.10)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\gamma}(K_{(2)j}^{\phantom{(2)}i}-\delta^{i}_{j}\lambda_{(2)})\xi^{j}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\Xi_{(2)}^{ji}\right)-\sqrt{\gamma}v_{(2)}^{i}\,,

(C.11)

the first of which one can check explicitly using equations (
4.31
) and (
4.37
), thus demonstrating that the zeroth order term in (
C.1
) vanishes identically. Plugging (
C.11
) back into (
C.1
) we find

I=\int_{\partial C}\partial_{l}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\Xi_{(2)}^{lk}\right)\varepsilon_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}=-\int_{\partial C}d*_{3}\mathbf{\Xi}=0\,,

(C.12)

where we used the fact that
\partial(\partial C)=\emptyset
. This completes the proof that (
C.1
) vanishes and demonstrates that the Hamiltonians are finite.

Appendix D
Variations of induced quantities

In the main text we make use of the variations of several of the induced quantities defined on the hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau}
, all which are induced by the variation of the bulk spacetime metric

\delta_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}\,.

(D.1)

Here we list several useful formulae for variations of induced quantities, making use of (
4.54
) for the components of the Lie derivative in the gauge (
4.13
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\xi}g_{ij}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{ij}=L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}+2K_{ij}\xi^{\tau}\,,

(D.2)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\sqrt{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ij}\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}g^{ij}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{ij}=\sqrt{\gamma}(D_{i}\xi^{i}+K\xi^{\tau})\,,

(D.3)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\gamma^{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\xi}g^{ij}=-g^{ki}g^{lj}\delta_{\xi}g_{kl}=-2\left(D^{(i}\xi^{j)}+K^{ij}\xi^{\tau}\right)\,,

(D.4)

\displaystyle 2\delta_{\xi}K_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\xi}\partial_{\tau}\gamma_{ij}=\left(\partial_{\tau}\delta_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}-\delta_{\dot{\xi}}\gamma_{ij}\right)=\partial_{\tau}(L_{\xi}\gamma_{ij}+2K_{ij}\xi^{\tau})-(L_{\dot{\xi}}\gamma_{ij}+2K_{ij}\dot{\xi}^{\tau})\,,

(D.5)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}K

\displaystyle=\delta_{\xi}\left(K_{ij}\gamma^{ij}\right)=\partial_{\tau}\left(D_{i}\xi^{i}+K\xi^{\tau}\right)-\left(D_{i}\dot{\xi}^{i}+\dot{\xi}^{\tau}K\right)\,,

(D.6)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}K\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{\tau}\partial_{\tau}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}K\right)+\partial_{i}\partial_{\tau}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\xi^{i}\right)-\sqrt{\gamma}D_{i}\dot{\xi}^{i}=\xi^{\tau}\partial_{\tau}\left(\sqrt{\gamma}K\right)+\partial_{i}\left(\xi^{i}\partial_{\tau}\sqrt{\gamma}\right)\,.

(D.7)

In particular, we note that the final variation is precisely the integrand of the GHY term that we consider in (
4.65
). The total derivative term does not contribute in (
4.65
) due to the compactness of
\Sigma_{\tau}
and thus one gets immediately the result presented in the main text.

Appendix E
Charge expression at
\mathscr{I}^{-}

We give a brief derivation of the Hamiltonian at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, indicating where the signs change from the charge at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We start with the Starobinsky expansion near the boundary of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
(setting
\ell=1
)

g=\frac{1}{\rho_{-}^{2}}\left(-d\rho_{-}^{2}+g^{-}_{(0)}+\rho_{-}^{2}g^{-}_{(2)}+\rho_{-}^{3}g^{-}_{(3)}+\ldots\right)\,,

(E.1)

where
\rho_{-}=0
is
\mathscr{I}^{-}
and
\rho_{-}>0
. The volume element in these coordinates is oriented as

\bm{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{3!}\sqrt{-g}\varepsilon_{ijk}d\rho_{-}\wedge dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\wedge dx^{k}\,,

(E.2)

and the integration is performed as

\int_{M^{-}}d^{4}x\,(\ldots)=\int_{0}d\rho_{-}^{\prime}\int_{\Sigma_{\rho_{-}^{\prime}}}d^{3}x\,(\ldots)\,.

(E.3)

Using the (orientation-reversing) diffeomorphism

\rho_{-}=e^{-\tau_{-}}\,,

(E.4)

the metric (
E.1
) becomes

g=-d\tau_{-}^{2}+e^{2\tau_{-}}g^{-}_{(0)}+g^{-}_{(2)}+e^{-\tau_{-}}g^{-}_{(3)}+\ldots\,,

(E.5)

where
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is at
\tau_{-}=\infty
and
\rho_{-}=\infty
is
\tau_{-}=-\infty
. Integration in these coordinates becomes

\int_{M^{-}}d^{4}x\,(\ldots)=-\int_{\infty}^{-\infty}d\tau_{-}^{\prime}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{-}^{\prime}}}d^{3}x\,(\ldots)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\tau_{-}^{\prime}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{-}^{\prime}}}d^{3}x\,(\ldots)\,,

(E.6)

where we note that the vector
\partial_{\tau_{-}}
is now
past-pointing
and so is outward-pointing from the perspective of
\mathscr{I}^{-}
embedded in the bulk spacetime. This makes the
\mathscr{I}^{-}
analysis computationally equivalent to the case of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as studied in Sections
4.1
through
4.4.3
with the replacements

g^{+}_{(0)}\rightarrow g^{-}_{(0)}\,,\qquad g^{+}_{(2)}\rightarrow g^{-}_{(2)}\,,\qquad g^{+}_{(3)}\rightarrow-g^{-}_{(3)}\,,

(E.7)

where we note in particular that the third term changes sign. These substitutions can be applied to all expressions at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in order to recover the analogous expressions at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
, in particular, if we define the
\mathscr{I}^{-}
energy-momentum tensor by analogy with equation (
4.41
) to be

\langle T_{ij}\rangle^{\mathscr{I}^{-}}={T}^{-}_{ij}=-\frac{3\ell}{16\pi G}g^{-}_{(3)ij}\,,

(E.8)

then the Hamiltonian associated with a one-ended hypersurface
C
which ends at
\mathscr{I}^{-}
is

H_{\xi}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{-k}_{\phantom{-}l}\xi_{(0)-}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)-}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\,,

(E.9)

which arises immediately from (
4.119
) and picks up an additional minus sign due to the sign flip in (
E.7
). We note that using these results we can immediately express the Hamiltonian associated with a
two-ended
hypersurface
C
as

H_{\xi}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\partial C^{+}}T^{+k}_{\phantom{+}l}\xi_{+(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)+}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}-\int_{\partial C^{-}}T^{-k}_{\phantom{-}l}\xi_{-(0)}^{l}\epsilon^{(0)-}_{kij}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\right)\,,

(E.10)

where
\partial C^{\pm}=C\cap\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
.

Appendix F
Pullback of the symplectic current to a spacelike hypersurface

Start from the generic expression for the pullback of the symplectic current

\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\psi)|_{I_{t}}=(\delta\pi^{ij}\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\gamma_{ij}-\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\pi^{ij}\delta\gamma_{ij})\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}\,,

(F.1)

for a generic vector
\zeta
defined on the full spacetime
g_{\mu\nu}
. We begin by noting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\gamma_{ij}&=L_{\zeta}\gamma_{ij}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\gamma}_{ij}=D_{i}\zeta_{j}+D_{j}\zeta_{i}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\gamma}_{ij}\,,\\
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\pi^{ij}&=L_{\zeta}\pi^{ij}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\pi}^{ij}=\zeta^{k}D_{k}\pi^{ij}-\pi^{ik}D_{k}\zeta^{j}-\pi^{kj}D_{k}\zeta^{i}+(D_{k}\zeta^{k})\pi^{ij}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\pi}^{ij}\,,\end{split}

(F.2)

where
D_{i}
is the covariant derivative compatible with
\gamma_{ij}
, and we recall that
\pi^{ij}
is a tensor density of weight
+1
. Applying these expressions to the symplectic current (
F.1
) gives us

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\psi)|_{I_{t}}&=\big(\delta\pi^{ij}[D_{i}\zeta_{j}+D_{j}\zeta_{i}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\gamma}_{ij}]\\
&\phantom{aaaaaa}-\delta\gamma_{ij}[D_{k}(\zeta^{k}\pi^{ij})-\pi^{ik}D_{k}\zeta^{j}-\pi^{kj}D_{k}\zeta^{i}+\zeta^{t}\dot{\pi}^{ij}]\big)\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}\,,\end{split}

(F.3)

at which point we want to use the ADM equations of motion (
4.19
) in order to substitute equations for
\dot{\gamma}_{ij}
and
\dot{\pi}^{ij}
as well as the linearised constraint equations in order to rearrange the terms involving
\delta\pi^{ij}
and
\delta\gamma_{ij}
. For the constraint equations, we follow the presentation of Appendix E.2 of
166
(the only difference here is that we have
\Lambda>0
) and they read

\displaystyle-R[\gamma]+4\kappa^{4}\gamma^{-1}

\displaystyle\pi^{ij}\pi_{ij}-2\kappa^{4}\gamma^{-1}\pi^{2}+2\Lambda=0\,,

(F.4)

\displaystyle D_{i}(\gamma^{-1/2}\pi^{ij})=0\,,

(F.5)

where
\pi=\pi^{i}_{i}
. This means that the linearised constraint equations are

\displaystyle R^{ij}[\gamma]\delta\gamma_{ij}+4\kappa^{4}\delta

\displaystyle(\gamma^{-1}\pi^{ij}\pi_{ij})-2\kappa^{4}\delta(\gamma^{-1}\pi^{2})=0\,,

(F.6)

\displaystyle D_{i}(\delta\pi^{ij})+\delta\Gamma^{j}_{ik}\pi^{ik}=0\,.

(F.7)

Similarly, the dynamical equations are given by

\displaystyle\dot{\gamma}_{ij}

\displaystyle=4\kappa^{2}\gamma^{-1/2}\left(\pi_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{ij}\pi\right)\,,

(F.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\kappa^{2}\dot{\pi}^{ij}&=-\gamma^{1/2}\left({R}^{ij}[\gamma]+2\kappa^{4}\gamma^{ij}\gamma^{-1}\pi^{2}-4\kappa^{4}\gamma^{ij}\gamma^{-1}\pi^{kl}\pi_{kl}\right)\\
&\qquad-8\kappa^{4}\gamma^{-1/2}\left(\pi^{ik}\pi_{k}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\pi\pi^{ij}\right)\,,\end{split}

(F.9)

where we have applied (
F.4
) in writing the evolution equation for
\pi^{ij}
, accounting for the differences between this and that found in
166
. The aim is to use these equations in order to prove
D
-exactness of the symplectic current, and be able to extract the
\delta
-integrable and
\delta
-non-integrable parts once the spacetime integration has been performed. Rearranging the expression for (
F.3
), and using the second of the linearised constraint equations (
F.7
), together with the identity

D_{k}(\delta\gamma_{ij})=\gamma_{lj}\delta\Gamma^{l}_{ki}-\gamma_{il}\delta\Gamma^{l}_{kj}\,,

(F.10)

we are able to re-express (
F.3
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\psi)|_{I_{t}}=\left(D_{i}(2\delta(\pi^{ij}\zeta_{j})-\zeta^{i}\pi^{jk}\delta\gamma_{jk})+\zeta^{t}[\delta\pi^{ij}\dot{\gamma}_{ij}-\delta\gamma_{ij}\dot{\pi}^{ij}]\right)\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}\,,\end{split}

(F.11)

upon which we use the evolution equations of motion (
F.8
), (
F.9
) together with the first linearised initial value constraint (
F.6
) in order to show that the final term proportional to
\zeta^{t}
is in fact zero, giving us a neat expression for the pullback of the symplectic current:

\bm{\omega}(\psi,\delta\psi,\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\psi)|_{I_{t}}=D_{i}\left(2\delta(\pi^{ij}\zeta_{j})-\zeta^{i}\pi^{jk}\delta\gamma_{jk}\right)\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}\,,

(F.12)

which matches equation (
4.158
) in the main text.

Appendix G
Flux formula for Bondi time translation in linearised RTdS

One of the examples of gravitational charges in Section
6.4
demonstrates that the Bondi mass is a monotonically decreasing charge at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. In order to arrive at this result, we made the choice of vector field (
6.85
), which is certainly not a unique choice of vector field satisfying the requirement (
6.48
).
In this appendix we consider a different choice of vector field, namely that of the
Bondi time translation

\xi=\partial_{u_{\text{B}}}\,,

(G.1)

where
u_{\text{B}}
is the
retarded Bondi time
coordinate
33
. This vector field was already used in
113
;
47
;
46
;
34
to define a notion of Bondi mass for AldS spacetimes, and thus by considering this vector field within our framework we will be able to compare our result with the existing literature.

We will follow
34
most closely, a work which considers
axisymmetric
RT solutions
linearised
around the SdS solution (see also
142
for a brief review of axisymmetric RT metrics with
\Lambda=0
). We begin by recalling their quadratic flux formula

\frac{dE}{du_{\text{B}}}=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16\pi G}\oint d^{2}S\,\left[\left(\dot{f}^{\prime\prime}-\cot\theta\dot{f}^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\frac{3m}{\ell^{2}}\partial_{u_{\text{B}}}\left(f^{\prime 2}\right)\right]\,,

(G.2)

where
\epsilon
is the perturbative parameter away from the Schwarzchild solution. We note that the expression above contains both a monotonically decreasing term (the first one) and a term which is not monotonically decreasing and depends on the cosmological constant
\Lambda=3/\ell^{2}
. In order to understand this formula within our framework, we first provide an explanation of the notation above. The function
f
is defined through the linearised map from RT into Bondi coordinates

\displaystyle\begin{split}u_{\text{RT}}&=u_{\text{B}}-\epsilon f(u_{\text{B}},\theta_{\text{B}})+\mathcal{O}(r_{\text{B}}^{-3})\,,\\
\theta_{\text{RT}}&=\theta_{\text{B}}+\mathcal{O}(r_{\text{B}}^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(G.3)

and we also note that
\dot{f}=\partial_{u_{\text{B}}}f
,
f^{\prime}=\partial_{\theta_{\text{B}}}f
which coincide to leading order with the same derivatives in the RT coordinates, hence the quadratic flux formula (
G.2
) can be taken to be read with either the Bondi or RT coordinates. We also note that
\oint d^{2}S=\int d\Omega_{0}=\int\sin\theta\,d\theta d\phi
,
i.e.
the integral over the round unit
S^{2}
.

It is of interest to see whether the Bondi time translation (
G.1
) recovers the flux formula (
G.2
) when input into our charge formula (
6.10
). We first express this vector field in terms of RT coordinates, which can be done schematically via

\partial_{u_{\text{B}}}=\frac{\partial x^{\mu}_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial u_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{u_{\text{RT}}}+\frac{\partial\theta_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{\theta_{\text{RT}}}+\frac{\partial r_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{r_{\text{RT}}}\,,

(G.4)

for the axisymmetric case. We then express the vector in FG coordinates via the maps (
6.11
), (
6.12
) obtaining

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{u_{\text{B}}}&=\frac{\partial u_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{t}+\frac{\partial\theta_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{\theta_{\text{RT}}}+\frac{\partial r_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{r_{\text{RT}}}\\
&=\frac{\partial u_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{t}+\frac{\partial\theta_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\partial_{\theta}-\frac{\partial r_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{\text{B}}}\frac{\partial r_{*}}{\partial r_{\text{RT}}}\partial_{\rho}+\mathcal{O}(\rho)\,,\end{split}

(G.5)

where the coordinates with no subscript correspond to boundary coordinates. We want to pull this vector field back to the boundary and since this is given by
\rho=\rho_{0}(\rightarrow 0)
we can ignore the
\partial_{\rho}
component as well as the
\mathcal{O}(\rho)
terms as we proceed. We are thus interested in computing the charge (
6.10
) defined by the vector field

\left.\partial_{u_{B}}\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}=\frac{\partial u_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{B}}\partial_{t}+\frac{\partial\theta_{\text{RT}}}{\partial u_{B}}\partial_{\theta}\,,

(G.6)

up to quadratic order in perturbation
\epsilon
around the SdS solution.

In order to do this, we use the non-linear transformation from RT to Bondi coordinates as given in Appendix B of
34
, which we write as

\displaystyle\begin{split}u_{\text{RT}}&=F(u_{B},\theta_{B})+\mathcal{O}(1/r_{B})\,,\\
\theta_{\text{RT}}&=H(u_{B},\theta_{B})+\mathcal{O}(1/r_{B})\,,\end{split}

(G.7)

where we can ignore the subleading terms in the Bondi radial coordinate as these will vanish as
\rho\rightarrow 0
. We note from
34
the constraints that must be satisfied by these functions

H^{\prime 2}+\frac{P^{2}F^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}=\csc^{2}\theta\sin^{2}H\,,

(G.8)

which comes from demanding that the leading order part of the angular metric in Bondi gauge is the unit round
S^{2}
metric, and

P(\dot{F}H^{\prime}-F^{\prime}\dot{H})=\csc^{2}\theta\sin^{2}H\,,

(G.9)

which comes from demanding the correct asymptotic behaviour of the
g_{uu}
component at leading order in Bondi gauge.
23
23

23

The asymptotics of the Bondi gauge in
34
is different from the ones of
e.g.

136
;
63
. More precisely,
34
has
g_{uA}=\mathcal{O}(r_{\text{B}}^{2})
but has
g_{AB}=r_{\text{B}}^{2}\mathring{\gamma}_{AB}+\mathcal{O}(r_{\text{B}})
, where
\mathring{\gamma}_{AB}
is the metric of the unit round
S^{2}
, whereas
63
uses the
\Lambda
-BMS gauge choice of
g_{AB}=r_{\text{B}}^{2}\gamma_{AB}+\mathcal{O}(r_{\text{B}})
, where
\sqrt{\gamma}=\sqrt{\mathring{\gamma}}
, but has
g_{uA}=\mathcal{O}(1)
. These correpsond to different choices of boundary gauge fixing. It would also be interesting to examine the charge corresponding to the
\Lambda
-BMS choice.
Note that
P=P(F(u_{B},\theta_{B}),H(u_{B},\theta_{B}))
and in matching our conventions we have
P=\hat{\sigma}=e^{(\hat{\Phi}_{0}-\hat{\Phi})/2}
. We note that the linear order of the map (
G.7
) can be read off from (
G.3
), with the result being

\displaystyle\begin{split}F(u_{B},\theta_{B})&=u_{B}-\epsilon f(u_{B},\theta_{B})\,,\\
H(u_{B},\theta_{B})&=\theta_{B}\,.\end{split}

(G.10)

We want to compute the mass aspect up to quadratic order in order to analyse the flux, so we want to extend the transformation above to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
. We start with the general form of the transformation

\displaystyle\begin{split}F(u_{B},\theta_{B})&=1-\epsilon f(u_{B},\theta_{B})+\epsilon^{2}f_{2}(u_{B},\theta_{B})\,,\\
H(u_{B},\theta_{B})&=\theta_{B}+\epsilon^{2}h_{2}(u_{B},\theta_{B})\,,\end{split}

(G.11)

and then use equations (
G.8
), (
G.9
) to solve for
f_{2},h_{2}
. We start with (
G.8
) and find at quadratic order in
\epsilon
the equation

\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}+2h_{2}^{\prime}-2h_{2}\cot\theta_{B}=0\,,

(G.12)

which in particular tells us that

\oint d^{2}S\,\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}=-4\oint d^{2}S\,h_{2}^{\prime}\,,

(G.13)

where we made use of integration by parts on the sphere. With this in hand, we now rewrite (
G.9
) as

\dot{F}=\frac{1}{PH^{\prime}}\left(H^{\prime 2}+\frac{P^{2}F^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}+PF^{\prime}\dot{H}\right)\,,

(G.14)

which is a useful result in evaluating the vector field since

\left.\partial_{u_{B}}\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}=\dot{F}\partial_{t}+\dot{H}\partial_{\theta}\,,

(G.15)

we note that since
\dot{H}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
and
\partial_{\theta}(\hat{\Delta}\hat{\Phi})=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
the angular contribution to the modified Hamiltonian (
6.49
) is
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})
and it is thus suitable to consider merely

\left.\partial_{u_{B}}\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}=\dot{F}\partial_{t}\,,

(G.16)

when computing up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
. Using this together with (
6.31
), we write the modified Hamiltonian as

\mathcal{H}_{\xi_{B}}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\int_{\partial C}\dot{F}e^{\hat{\Phi}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}}\,d\Omega_{0}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\frac{1}{P^{3}}\left(H^{\prime}+\frac{P^{2}F^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}H^{\prime}}+\frac{PF^{\prime}\dot{H}}{H^{\prime}}\right)\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.17)

and now expanding the round bracket above up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
we find

\mathcal{H}_{\xi_{B}}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\frac{1}{P^{3}}\left(1+\epsilon^{2}\left[\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}+h_{2}^{\prime}\right]\right)\,d^{2}S=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\frac{1}{P^{3}}\left(1+\epsilon^{2}\frac{3}{4}\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.18)

which is a promising-looking
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
correction to the monotonically decreasing quantity given in (
6.90
).

In order to compare the above expression directly with (
G.2
), we first analyse the monotonically decreasing part. We begin with the expression for the “Bondi mass” part of the modified Hamiltonian

\mathcal{M}_{B}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\frac{1}{P^{3}}\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.19)

and we wish to look at the boundary time derivative of this quantity

\partial_{t}\mathcal{M}_{B}=-\frac{6m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\frac{\dot{P}}{P^{4}}\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.20)

clearly the important quantity above is
\dot{P}
, which we can compute using the quadratic approximation as

P=1+\epsilon p+\epsilon^{2}p_{2}\,,\implies\dot{P}=\epsilon\dot{p}+\epsilon^{2}\dot{p}_{2}\,.

(G.21)

In order to solve for
\dot{p},\dot{p}_{2}
, we again use the RT equation (
6.4
), written in the notation of
34
as

3m\frac{\dot{P}}{P}+\frac{1}{8}\Delta_{h}R_{h}=0\,,

(G.22)

where
R_{h}
is the Ricci scalar of
h=P^{-2}d\Omega_{2}^{2}
and
\Delta_{h}
is the Laplacian of the same metric. Solving this equation at
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
gives

\displaystyle\dot{p}=-\frac{1}{12m}\left(p^{(4)}+\cot\theta\left(2p^{\prime\prime\prime}-\cot\theta p^{\prime\prime}+\left(\csc^{2}\theta+2\right)p^{\prime}\right)\right)\,,

(G.23)

and the expression for
\dot{p}_{2}
is a little lengthy but arises in the same way by solving the Robinson-Trautman equation at
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
. We note that up to quadratic order the time derivative of the Bondi mass gives

\partial_{t}\mathcal{M}_{B}=\frac{2m}{\kappa^{2}}\oint\left[-3\epsilon\dot{p}-3\epsilon^{2}\left(\dot{p}_{2}-4p\dot{p}\right)\right]\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.24)

now using (
G.23
) and several integrations by parts, we see that the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
term above vanishes. The
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
term gives

\partial_{t}\mathcal{M}_{B}=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2\kappa^{2}}\oint\left(p^{\prime\prime}-\cot(\theta)p^{\prime}\right)^{2}\,d^{2}S=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16\pi G}\oint\left(\dot{f}^{\prime\prime}-\cot(\theta)\dot{f}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.25)

which is precisely the monotonically decreasing part of (
G.2
). This means that our flux formula for the modified Hamiltonian (
G.18
) is

\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}_{{\xi}_{B}}}=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16\pi G}\oint\left(\left(\dot{f}^{\prime\prime}-\cot(\theta)\dot{f}^{\prime}\right)^{2}-3m\partial_{t}\left(\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\right)\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.26)

which differs from (
G.2
) but agrees with the flux formulae presented in
47
;
113
;
46
.

Clearly there is some discrepancy in the literature concerning the precise flux formula for RT waves in de Sitter spacetime. However, we note that all formulae discussed so far are consistent at the level of the total energy radiated through the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, a point which was already noted in
34
when providing comparison with
15
;
113
;
46
. We will now recount this argument for our Robinson-Trautman flux formula, arguing that all quadratic formulae result in overall non-positive flux of energy, assuming some basic properties on the falloff of the function
f^{\prime}
. We begin by considering the total energy radiated through the boundary

\Delta\mathcal{H}_{\xi_{B}}=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16\pi G}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}dt\,\oint\left(\left(\dot{f}^{\prime\prime}-\cot(\theta)\dot{f}^{\prime}\right)^{2}-3m\partial_{t}\left(\frac{f^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\right)\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.27)

then we perform the
t
-integral over the total derivative term and use the fact that the spacetime must settle down to SdS as
t\rightarrow\infty
which tells us
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}f^{\prime}=0
, together with the assumption
f^{\prime}(t_{0})=0
. Putting these facts together we find

\Delta\mathcal{H}_{\xi_{B}}=-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16\pi G}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}dt\,\oint\left(\dot{f}^{\prime\prime}-\cot(\theta)\dot{f}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\,d^{2}S\,,

(G.28)

which is manifestly non-positive. This argument shows that the linearised charge corresponding to the Bondi time translation vector field in RTdS spacetime admits a loss of energy through
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, a sensible result on physical grounds.
